**Hermetic Philosophy**

<note>This central bookplate belongs to a collection dedicated to the "Hermetic Philosophy," a tradition of spiritual knowledge. The emblem shows a pelican "in its piety," a classic symbol where the mother bird feeds her chicks with her own blood; in an esoteric context, this represents the sacrifice and refinement required in the alchemical process. The three roses on the base and the sunburst containing a triangle (divinity) and a dove (the soul or spirit) suggest a connection to Rosicrucian thought.</note>

<meta>This page serves as the formal title page for a commemorative work or funeral poem dedicated to Caroline of Hesse-Darmstadt (1721–1774). Caroline, known as "The Great Landgravine," was a highly respected intellectual of her era, corresponding with figures like Voltaire and Goethe. The text uses the traditional German Fraktur script and follows the standard formatting for 18th-century noble eulogies.</meta>

->dwelling,<-

<meta>Continuing from page 3. The previous page ended with "Where the garment of mortality decays," which this page completes: "That once surrounded a noble lady." The catchword "The" from the previous page aligns with the opening of this thought.</meta>

That once surrounded a noble lady,
Who, in the shell of a mortal,
Trod the path of life.
Gentle Moon,
You confidant of Wisdom!
In all distant centuries, you have
Shone upon the pious,
And with your cleansing beams
Enlightened the eye of contemplation.
How silently you glide over my head,
As if you feared
To disturb my laboring soul.
Your light you spread softly over the urn,
Which Frederick, the Only, <note>A reference to Frederick II (the Great) of Prussia (reigned 1740–1786), often called "Friedrich der Einzige" by his admirers.</note>
Placed for the Transfigured One. <note>In 18th-century elegiac poetry, "the Transfigured" (die Verklärte) refers to the deceased person, now perfected in the afterlife.</note>
Masculine and great was the character of the one at rest; <note>In this period, "masculine" (männlich) was a high compliment for a woman, signifying strength of mind, stoicism, and intellectual rigor rather than a comment on gender identity.</note>
She combined goodness of heart
With courage and spirit,
And determination and heroism
Were the traits of her soul.
Her intellect was piercing, and quick to grasp.
Extensive knowledge adorned her mind,
And well-ordered reading her soul;
She was charitable, and knew the value
Of suffering humanity.
Her charitable deeds resemble the gifts of spring,

<meta>Continuing from page 3. The previous page ended by comparing the deceased lady's charitable deeds to the "gifts of spring." This page continues that metaphor, personifying Spring as a creative force before returning to the lady's transition into the afterlife. Note: The OCR identifies "Frey" as the catchword, though the main text begins with "Dessen" (Whose), suggesting a potential missing leaf or a discrepancy in the original binder's catchword.</meta>

Whose creative existence
Surrounds every region with joy
That his steps wander through.
He clothes the dry shrub with young foliage,
The meadows with flowers.
His warming breath
Thaws the cold from the mountain, and melts the
->chains,<-
That bound the brooks with ice; and so he gives
->to the sun,<-
The mirrors of nature—the springs and brooks—
->back.<-

Like a benevolent deity,
The righteous lady <note>original: "die Gerechte" (the righteous woman). In the context of 18th-century funeral oratory, this implies she lived a life of moral perfection.</note> walked,
Who slumbers here under this burial mound,
Through the valleys of life.
Good deeds follow her
Into the fields of eternity.
There she laid aside the garment of mortality <note>A common Enlightenment-era metaphor for the physical body, viewing death as the soul discarding an earthly costume to return to its true state.</note>,
And, waiting upon the goodness of the Deity,
Full of silent expectation,
She descended undaunted
Into the vaults of death.
Silent solitude!
Teach me here to know the value of life!
Here she rests, the rosebud <note>original: "Rosenknospe." This imagery suggests that while she has died, her beauty and soul remain "in bud," waiting to bloom in the afterlife.</note>,
Guarded by protective angels.

<meta>Continuing from page 3 (noted as page 5 in sequence). The previous page concluded with the image of the deceased lady as a "rosebud" resting in the vaults of death, guarded by angels. This page continues the meditation on her spiritual transition.</meta>

Free from the storms of life
She unfolds into a flower
For the garden of eternity.
In her, the Deity is now mirrored;
In her, the image of the Eternal is now mirrored,
And He recognizes His essence, which was His own likeness. <note>original: "Ebenbild." A reference to the biblical concept in Genesis that humanity is created in the image and likeness of God.</note>
O, what do I imagine here! What a feeling! What a
->premonition!<-
Here, where the world sees nothing, my spirit sees
->everything;<-
Here, where the fearful shrink back
And tremble at the word, decay, <note>original: "Verwesung" (decomposition/corruption). In 18th-century funerary texts, this word emphasizes the physical reality of death which the soul eventually overcomes.</note>
Here I first feel that I live,
And that for the soul there is no death.
Here, what the world calls great becomes small to me,
And what it despises becomes great.
The development of higher powers is indeed not death.
The fragile hut falls to ruin, <note>original: "zerbrechliche Hütte." A common metaphor in this period for the physical body, viewing it as a temporary shelter for the eternal soul.</note>
For which we paid interest dearly enough, <note>The author uses a financial metaphor: the "interest" paid on the physical body is the suffering, effort, and aging endured during life.</note>
And the daughter of heaven
Soars up to the dwellings of bliss,
Which are destined for her.
Venerable is still the dust she inhabited;
For the dust enclosed her, the immortal one,
And was hallowed through deeds,
Through the beautiful deeds of life.
Even you, dust, are now already more noble

On the level of progression.
Transfiguration awaits you on the great day of
->recompense.<- <note>original: "Wiedervergeltung." While this can mean retribution or vengeance in modern German, in this 18th-century religious context, it refers to the "day of reckoning" or the final divine reward/repayment for one's life.</note>

->of mortals.<-
Even the lily may not clothe itself with you;
You are holy, preserved for the wedding garment. <note>original: "hochzeitlichen Kleidung." A biblical allusion to the "wedding garment" required for the heavenly banquet in the Parable of the Wedding Feast (Matthew 22).</note>

->vaults<-
Of the universe,
Is permitted to borrow this dust,
When he presents himself visibly to our coarser organs, <note>original: "gröbern Organen." This refers to the physical human senses, which were thought to be too "coarse" to perceive pure spirit without the spirit taking on some material form.</note>
As a proclaimer of the truth of eternity.
But hush! — the winds blow,
The foliage of the trees moves over the grave:
What a feeling! —
Be blessed by me, you precious moments
Of the midnight hour!
You call me to the school of learning.
Here, at the gateway of eternity,
Decay teaches truth and virtue.

> Almighty Deity!
> Let me bow my knees deep before you,
> You worked the good in the transfigured one. <note>original: "Verklärten." One who has undergone transfiguration (Verklärung), passing from a mortal state to a glorified, heavenly state.</note>
> The Deity destined you, blessed one! for the throne,
> And the shimmer of the throne never blinded your
->eye,<-

<term>Transfiguration</term> (Verklärung): The process of being transformed into a glorified, heavenly state after death. <term>Recompense</term> (Wiedervergeltung): The final divine settlement or reward for a person’s earthly actions. <term>Eternity</term> (Ewigkeit): The timeless state of existence after death. <term>Decay</term> (Verwesung): The physical decomposition of the body, which the author views here as a teacher of moral truth. <term>Deity</term> (Gottheit): A formal term for God. <term>Blessed One</term> (Selige): A term for the deceased, implying they are now in a state of heavenly bliss.

<meta>Continuing from the previous page, which described the deceased woman as being "destined for the throne" yet unblinded by its light, this page explores her spiritual detachment from the trappings of power and her devotion to her role as a mother and wife.</meta>

And the shackles of splendor never weighed upon your free-
->born spirit.<-
In vain did earthly majesty seek your favor,
In vain did she smile upon you—she, the traitress,
Who binds kings in chains,
And punishes their lustfulness with slavery.
With serene abstinence <note>original: "gelassener Enthaltsamkeit." In an 18th-century context, this suggests a stoic and pious self-control that remains untroubled by worldly temptations.</note>
You only lightly touched with innocent lips
The cup of human joys.
Your soul thirsted for purer springs,
For springs that the earth does not give.
Your virtue conquered the passions of greatness
That insidiously lie in wait around thrones,
That turn kings into beggars
Who beg for the alms of earthly joy from the
->deceivers.<- <note>This likely refers to the personified vanities of courtly life that promised happiness but delivered only spiritual emptiness.</note>

Born for eternity,
You walked like a traveler,
Full of jealous care for your dignity
Through the pilgrimage of this life. <note>original: "Wanderschaft." While it literally means a journey or wandering, in this religious context it refers to the "earthly pilgrimage" of a soul toward heaven.</note>
The fulfillment of the most sacred duties
Was bliss for your feeling heart:
To be a wife and mother,
To form your children into human beings. <note>The phrase "to form children into human beings" (Menschen zu bilden) was a key ideal of the Enlightenment, meaning to cultivate their moral character, reason, and empathy.</note>
Permeated by this thought,
You raised your eye with each new day as you awoke

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 8, this page completes the sentence regarding the subject’s daily devotion, describing how her spiritual clarity informed her role as a mother and a virtuous leader.</meta>

And beheld in every creature the imprint of the
Godhead. <note>original: "Abdruck der Gottheit." This reflects the 18th-century "physico-theological" view that the divine could be discerned through the rational study of nature and the physical world.</note>
You wisely counted your hours,
And your heart surrendered to wisdom.
The eye of your soul read deep in the book of cre-
ation,
And you saw nothing through the deceptive glass of earth-
ly desires. <note>original: "täuschende Glas irdischer Wünsche." A metaphor suggesting that worldly cravings act like a distorted lens or "looking glass," warping one's perception of spiritual truth.</note>
For spheres of a higher kind you formed your children
Into princely children and human beings, <note>The text highlights a key Enlightenment ideal: that those of high rank must first be cultivated as "Menschen" (human beings) with moral character and empathy before they can properly fulfill their royal duties.</note>
And pressed the seal of great sentiments
Early into their feeling hearts.
Steadfast and enduring you were in all
That your great soul designed.
Active willpower was your goodness,
Not mere impulse; <note>original: "Nicht blose Impulsion." This implies that her virtue was a disciplined, rational choice and a strength of character rather than a fleeting or accidental emotion.</note>
Linked with wisdom
Was every one of your undertakings,
And drew to you admiration and respect.
Thanks be to you, immortal one!
Bliss is your reward in the fields of eternity.
Here, where you rest, is a temple
Built for the Eternal One.
Sacred is this place, consecrated with tears of
emotion,
Hallowed by tears of gratitude.

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page's solemn reflection at Laura’s grave, this page transitions into a lyrical, retrospective description of Laura in her youth, portraying her as a paragon of natural beauty and virtue before her "transfiguration."</meta>

# The Blessedness of Virtue.

Laura was beautiful; she was in bloom
Like an unfolding rose, on whose purple petals
The sun reflects in the morning dew.
Her nut-brown hair flowed majestically
Down her white neck.
The Graces <note>In Greek mythology, the Graces (or Charites) are the goddesses of charm, beauty, and nature; here, they are credited with shaping her physical perfection.</note> shaped her slender stature, and the tone of her voice
Was the melody of nature.
Joyfully she skipped, like a lively deer,
In the shaded valley, where ambrosial <note>original: "ambrosische." Referring to the fragrance or food of the gods, used here to describe a divine or heavenly scent.</note> scents
Of herbs wafted.
The balsamic <note>Refers to a soothing, aromatic, and resinous fragrance typical of certain plants.</note> fragrance of spring flowers
Was carried by flickering west winds <note>original: "Westwinde." In classical poetry, the West Wind (Zephyrus) is the gentle wind of springtime.</note> on light wings
Toward the rising sun,
As a gift to the awakening morning.
Laura had already celebrated seventeen returning Mays
In the month of roses, <note>original: "Rosenmond." An archaic and poetic German name for the month of June.</note>
For seventeen times the tall linden tree had bloomed
By the cottage of Laura's parents,
Since the Deity gave this gift of love
To those honest folk.
The sun rose, and solemnly there smoked

<meta>catchword: The <note>original: "Die." This catchword suggests the next page continues with a description of rising smoke, likely from a sacrifice or a hearth: "Die Opfer" (The offerings/sacrifices).</note></meta>

<summary>This page presents a poetic idealized portrait of Laura at age seventeen, emphasizing her harmony with nature and her role as a divine gift to her parents. It uses classical imagery to establish her as a symbol of youthful innocence and virtue.</summary>

<keywords>Laura, beauty, nature, Graces, youth, June, linden tree, virtue, pastoral poetry</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>Continuing from the description of the sunrise over the rural landscape and Laura’s childhood home, this page captures her spiritual response to the beauty of nature as she offers a prayer of gratitude.</meta>

The mountains in the surrounding area;
Just as once upon Abel’s altar <note>A biblical reference to the Book of Genesis; Abel, the son of Adam, offered a sacrifice from his flock that was pleasing to God. The smoke here symbolizes a pure, ascending prayer.</note>
The thank-offering smoked.
With a thankful heart, Laura fixed
Her soulful eye toward heaven.
Bliss crept into her innocent heart. —
The blooming meadow and the laughing grain field,
Mingled with peaceful rural cottages,
Delighted her astonished gaze.
She felt the full glory of the spring morning:
With holy rapture she stood there, speechless and
marveling.

A thousand tender tears gave vent to her feelings.
With hands raised high, she,
The innocent one, prayed thus to heaven:

> “Thanks be to You! — To You, the Giver of my life! —
> “Thanks be to You for the bliss of this morning! —
> “Beautiful is Your world, which You have created:
> “But even more beautiful is the human countenance,
> “When no vice has yet desecrated its features,
> “And when peace and virtue are still mirrored
> within it.
> “In its springtime, <note>original: "Lenze." An archaic German poetic term for spring.</note> in its youthful adornment
> “Laughing nature still remains:
> “And I too, destined, one day upon the paths of
> virtue
> “To come to You, Immortal One!

# Feelings of Melancholy
## at the Corpse of a Beloved Wife.

---

Nightly silence reigns in this desolate region,
Like the silence of the lonely hours of midnight.
Sadly the <term>funeral lamp: "Todtenlampe," a lamp kept burning in a tomb or near a body, symbolizing the flickering life or the soul's light in the darkness of death.</term> spreads its gloomy light
In this dark vault.
A cold shudder flows through every one of my limbs:
My steps falter, and a hideous fear
Takes possession of my heart.
But why does my foot tremble on the path,
Where it glides?
Why does sweat bead from my forehead?
Am I then not here—here where the end of all
Misery is? —
Away with you, you phantoms of terror! —
Tyrants created you. <note>The speaker suggests that the "horrors" of death are artificial constructs used by powerful figures to inspire fear, rather than the true nature of death itself.</note> — Friendlily smiles
->Death,<-
Not hideous for him, who long already
->sought his friendship.<- <note>original: "seine Freundschaft suchte." This personifies Death as a welcome companion or a friend to the suffering.</note>
Come, reach me your hand, last companion of the
->mortal! —<-
And take from me the thanks of the suffering one,
Whom you with kindness out of the <term>dungeon: "Kerker." A common 18th-century metaphor for the physical body or the mortal world, portraying death as a liberation.</term> of this life
->leadest.<-
Your gaze seems already more gracious to me,

<summary>This page contains a poetic meditation on death, shifting from an initial fear of the dark vault to an acceptance of Death as a "kindly" friend who liberates the soul from the "dungeon" of life. It reflects the 18th-century transition from macabre views of death toward a more sentimental, welcoming perspective on the afterlife.</summary>
<keywords>melancholy, death as a friend, funeral lamp, funeral vault, liberation from suffering, Enlightenment poetry, mortality</keywords>

Already your eye is gentler. —
Why then should I tremble before you?

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>This page marks a tonal shift from the previous page's frantic denial toward a philosophical, though no less painful, reflection on the nature of death. Your eye is already gentler.—

Why then should I tremble before you?

Are you not the friend of humanity? <term>friend of humanity: "Menschenfreund." In the 18th-century context, this term personifies Death not as a "Grim Reaper," but as a benevolent figure who ends earthly suffering, a common theme in the literature of Sensibility (Empfindsamkeit).</term>

Who, but you, brings virtue to its reward?

Who, but you, unites us with our friends?

Who, but you, leads man to his destiny?—

Yes, like his brother, gentle sleep,

Death brings the mortal burdened with misery

Into the arms of rest.

From the cold lips of the dying man

He gently withdraws the exhausted chalice of human suffering.

But—what reveals itself here to my eye?—

Elmire!—Shrouded in the clothes of the dead!—— <note>original: "Todtenkleide." This refers to a burial shroud or the specific garments prepared for a corpse.</note>

Elmire—is it you?—You, most beloved of mortals!

What!—This is you!—Within this unfeeling body

Once lived the tenderest of souls.

Who has plucked the roses from your cheeks?

Who has fettered your hot lips with ice?—

Dark night covers your soul-filled eye.

No kiss, which once gave you spirit,

Wakes your gaze to life anymore.

Elmire!—Your ear is deaf to the voice of your husband! <note>original: "Gatten." This confirms the speaker is Elmire's husband, grounding the philosophical mourning in personal tragedy.</note>

# Poverty.

## A Portrait. <note>original: "Gemälde." In 18th-century literature, this term refers to a "word-picture" or "tableau"—a vivid, descriptive scene intended to provoke a strong emotional response and moral reflection in the reader.</note>

---

Densely overgrown with moss
Is the decayed board
That forms the shelter of this hut.
Rotten pillars are the weak supports
Of unfurnished walls, <term>geräthlos: Literally "without tools" or "without equipment." This suggests a home stripped of even the most basic furniture or household necessities.</term>
Through which the winds howl hideously. —
Cold dampness covers the muddy floor,
And fills with insects and filth the wretched dwelling
Of the suffering poor man.
Here, where the rain soaks through the poor rags
That cover his miserable limbs,
And the burning sun scorches his bare crown; <note>original: "Scheitel." The top or crown of the head, suggesting the man has no hat or protection from the elements.</note>
Here, where no beneficial tree spreads its
Shade,
No dry grass invites the weary to rest,
Here, where perhaps no snake would wish to dwell,
Here lives a human being. —
Who are you, poor creature? — who has thus 
        banished you from
Humanity? —
Come here, brother and fellow human, <note>original: "Mitmensch." A key concept in German Enlightenment thought, emphasizing the inherent dignity and social bonds that connect all people regardless of their economic status.</note>
And stand upon this hill! —

<meta>This page continues the poem "Poverty" from page 29. The narrator, having invited a "fellow human" to look upon a suffering man, now addresses the impoverished man directly, reflecting on the injustice of his condition compared to the natural abundance of the world.</meta>

I want to show you to your brothers.
The paleness of death ravages your features,
And the grief that disfigures your brow,
Proclaims the suffering of your soul
In a terrible expression.
You! — so it is you, who, forsaken by his
brothers,
Lives lonely in this valley of the world! — <note>original: "Weltthale." A poetic term (Valley of the World) framing earthly life as a place of trial or pilgrimage, often used in 18th-century literature to emphasize the hardships of existence.</note>
Lives as if nature had excluded you from the rights
Of humanity.
Brother! O tell me, what heaped this misery upon
Your noble head? —
Who snatched away your food,
Who your clothing, which covered your limbs?
Did not God and nature give to every human
Equal rights? — <note>original: "Gleiche Rechte." This reflects the influence of Enlightenment philosophy, which argued that all people possess inherent, natural rights regardless of their social standing.</note>
Why are you so excluded from these very things? —
Is not the vast expanse of this earth
Fruitful enough to nourish us all?
Who is it that dared to tear away
Your portion from you? —
Look around! Here hangs refreshing fruit
On densely laden trees,
And beckons you toward recovery.
Why do you hesitate to pluck the fruit
That smiles toward your lips?

To forge for ourselves the shackles of misery.
How my gaze shudders at the terrifying
caves,
Where eternal darknesses reign,
As in the vestibules of Orcus. <note>original: "Vorhöfen des Orkus." In Roman mythology, Orcus was a god of the underworld; the author uses this to describe the hellish, life-extinguishing environment of the mines.</note>
Here into these fearful abysses
Paid slaves descend <note>original: "beſoldete Sklaven." The author critiques wage labor in the mining industry by equating it to slavery, suggesting that even if "paid," the trade of one's life and health for metal is a form of bondage.</note>
To the shame of humanity,
And renounce the light of the sun,
To search for metals,
And dig up misfortune and poverty
Along with them.
Money is the idol of the earth;
To this, one sacrifices innocence and happiness.
Where are they now, the days of joy and
peace,
Which once bloomed so gloriously in the lands of Eden! — <note>Eden refers to the biblical Garden of Eden, representing a lost state of communal, natural perfection before the advent of greed.</note>
They are no more! — vanished only like a beautiful dream
They have disappeared, and lie buried
Beneath the immeasurable ruins
Of centuries rolled away.

## A Painting after Nature.

---

Brotherly love! You, child of the Divinity! <note>original: "Gottheit." In the 18th-century Enlightenment context, this refers to a universal divine force often associated with nature and moral truth.</note>
Where shall I seek you?
Oh, how happy was the earth,
When you still dwelt among us
In friendly bliss! <note>original: "Wonne." A key term in the "Sturm und Drang" (Storm and Stress) literary movement, denoting an intense, rapturous joy that is both emotional and spiritual.</note>
The human eye smiled gently,
And blessed peace
Flowed from expressive gazes.
The friend’s arm still entwined itself
Around the neck of the brother,
Upon whom the brother leaned.
The lover dried the sweat of melancholy <note>original: "Wehmuth." A specific type of sentimental sadness or wistful longing highly valued in 18th-century "Sentimentalism" (Empfindsamkeit) as a sign of a refined soul.</note>
From the brow of the sufferer.
And the tears of sorrow
Were kissed away
From the clouded eye
Of the weeping brother.
One soul still gathered
The feelings of all
Into one bosom. <note>In this period, the "bosom" (Busen) was considered the metaphorical seat of the soul and the center of genuine human emotion.</note>
Divine harmonies, audible only
To tenderer souls,
Whispered low notes of joy

<term>Brotherly love (Bruderliebe)</term>, <term>Divinity (Gottheit)</term>, <term>Melancholy (Wehmuth)</term>, <term>Harmonies (Harmonien)</term>, <term>Souls (Seelen)</term>

<summary>This page presents a poetic personification of Brotherly Love as a divine child, nostalgically recalling a "Golden Age" where human empathy and emotional unity were universal. It emphasizes the physical and spiritual connection between individuals through shared suffering and joy.</summary>

## A Portrait from Nature.
---

Brotherly love! You, the child of the Godhead!
Where shall I seek you?
Oh, how happy was the earth
When you still dwelt among us
In friendly bliss!
Softly the human eye smiled,
And blessed peace
Flowed from speaking glances.
The friend's arm still wrapped
Around the neck of the brother,
To whom the brother clung.
The lover dried the sweat of melancholy <note>original: "Wehmuth." A central concept in 18th-century German literature, Wehmuth describes a gentle, reflective sadness that is seen as a sign of a refined and virtuous soul.</note>
From the brow of the sufferer.
And the tears of sorrow
Were kissed from the clouded eye
Of the weeping brother.
One soul still gathered
The feelings of all
Into one bosom.
Divine harmonies, audible only
To more delicate souls,
Whispered soft notes of joy

<term>Brotherly love (Bruderliebe)</term>: In this context, it refers to "Philanthropy" or universal love for one's fellow man, a key ideal of the Enlightenment.
<term>Godhead (Gottheit)</term>: A poetic way of referring to the Divine or the creator.
<term>Bliss (Wonne)</term>: An intense, often spiritual state of happiness or delight.
<term>Harmonies (Harmonien)</term>: A reference to the "Harmony of the Spheres" or the belief that the universe—and human relationships—should function like a perfectly tuned musical instrument.

<summary>This page is a lyrical lament for a lost "golden age" of human empathy and connection, personifying Brotherly Love as a divine child who once lived among humanity. It uses physical imagery—embraces, kisses, and shared tears—to illustrate a state of perfect social and spiritual harmony.</summary>

<keywords>Brotherly love, empathy, Sentimentalism, Wehmuth, divine harmony, human connection, Enlightenment ideals</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the speaker’s lament from page 38 regarding the banishment of "Brotherly Love." The sentence begun on the previous page concludes here, transitioning from the internal state of the soul to a graphic description of the external horrors of war.</meta>

Would flee into the night of death —
When I think of you, exiled one. <note>The "exiled one" refers to personified Brotherly Love, mentioned on the previous page.</note>
A spirit of persecution reigns over this expanse
Of the earth;
Brothers arm themselves against brothers,
And dagger and poison arm the hands
Of malicious mortals.
Here, predatory armies devastate
The blossoming seed — and the hope of the country-
man.
Blood reddens the desecrated streams,
Which once flowed blissfully through the meadows.
Like thunder,
A devastating lead <note>original: "Bley." This refers to lead bullets or musket balls.</note>
Spews from brazen throats, <note>A poetic eighteenth-century metaphor for the muzzles of bronze cannons.</note>
And robs hundreds of their lives;
Life — which the greatest of kings
Cannot give even to a gnat.
Here, amid monstrous devastation,
A glowing bomb
Soars up toward Olympus. <note>The poet references the home of the Greek gods to illustrate the terrifying height and hubris of the explosive shells.</note>
Shattered, it spits destruction around itself
From a fiery maw;
It topples temples and palaces into the dust,
And buries honest citizens
Beneath the ruins
Of their friendly hearths. <note>original: "Heerd." The hearth or fireplace represented the heart of the home and family safety.</note>

<term>Spirit of persecution (Verfolgungsgeist)</term>: A term used to describe a social climate of intolerance and organized violence.
<term>Brazen throats (ehrenen Schlünden)</term>: In the poetry of this era, "brazen" (made of brass or bronze) was a standard epithet for cannons and artillery.
<term>Hearth (Heerd)</term>: More than just a fireplace, the hearth symbolized the sanctity of domestic life and "Gemütlichkeit" (coziness/belonging).

<summary>This page presents a stark indictment of war, detailing how the loss of "brotherly love" leads to the violent destruction of nature, life, and the home. The poet uses vivid imagery of artillery and bombs to contrast human destructiveness with the fragile value of life.</summary>
<keywords>war, persecution, artillery, weaponry, destruction, brotherly love, pacifism, domestic life</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the speaker’s graphic condemnation of war and the hollow pursuit of military glory. There bleeds in the arms of his tender wife
At the sacrificial altar <note>original: "Opferheerde." Literally a "sacrificial hearth," here used metaphorically to describe the site where lives are given up for a cause.</note> of false honor
The armored man in full harness;
Still his sword is reddened with the blood of his brothers,
Who never did him any harm.
Rattling <note>original: "Röchelnd." This refers to the "death rattle," the sound made by a person near death.</note> he dies — and his extinguished eye
Still squints enviously toward the hostile laurel, <note>The laurel wreath was the classical Roman symbol of victory and military triumph.</note>
Which encircles his pale temples.
He forgets the bonds of friendship,
Tears apart the chains of love,
Turns his heart into a tiger's, and his soul into a wolf's, <note>original: "Vertigert... verwölft." These are powerful poetic inventions by the author, meaning to take on the predatory and savage nature of these animals.</note>
To be entirely a warrior. —
Death, then, is your glory — death your honor,
You heroes! —
Ah, how your laurels -- yet beautifully upon your brow
->shine,<-
Stained with human blood,
Acquired for gold, and detested
By plundered lands.
O unhappy honor! — loathsome prejudice
Of centuries!
No human deed ever ennobled you;
Nothing saves you from the contempt
Of the wise man. —
Is the hero happier at the end of life,
Who murders a thousand, and yet cannot save himself from the steel
Of death?

<term>Sacrificial altar (Opferheerde)</term>: A poetic term linking the domestic "hearth" (Heerd) mentioned on the previous page with the "altar" of sacrifice. It suggests that the home is being sacrificed for war.
<term>To turn into a tiger/wolf (Vertigert/verwölft)</term>: These are "ver- " prefixed verbs in German that denote a transformation. The poet suggests that the profession of a "warrior" requires a literal loss of humanity, turning a man into a beast of prey.
<term>Prejudice (Vorurtheil)</term>: In the context of the Enlightenment, a "prejudice" was any belief held due to tradition or emotion rather than reason. Here, the poet calls the cult of military glory a "loathsome prejudice."

<summary>This page concludes a visceral anti-war poem, portraying a dying soldier who has traded his humanity and family for the "false honor" of a laurel wreath. It argues that military glory is a blood-stained "prejudice" that holds no value to a truly wise person.</summary>
<keywords>critique of war, false honor, military glory, dehumanization, pacifism, Enlightenment morality</keywords>

--- Page 64 ---
<meta>This page continues the speaker's critique of military glory, moving from the physical gore of the battlefield to the philosophical blindness of the soldier. It concludes with a sharp transition, suggesting that the "demon" of war has a peacetime equivalent in the form of cold, unfeeling legalism.</meta>

O warriors, let yourselves not be blinded 
By the midday sun of your days; 
Count also the morning and evening 
As part of the day. <note>The author uses the times of day as a metaphor for the stages of life, warning that the "midday" of youthful glory is fleeting and must be weighed against the beginning and end of one's existence.</note>
The whole sum of your boundless reputation 
Is a fairy tale, <note>original: "Mährchen." While we think of fairy tales as children's stories, the author uses it here to mean a fabrication or a hollow myth.</note>
Composed of glory and shame. — 
But the voice of humanity is in vain, 
It does not penetrate armored hearts, 
And the gentle ray of reason 
Slides off the heads 
That are covered with iron helmets. 
Only when you, rolling in the dust, 
Will bleed and die — ! 
Will you recognize the false ladder of your bliss <note>original: "Glückseligkeit." This refers to a state of supreme happiness or blessedness, here presented as a deceptive path the soldier climbs.</note> 
Upon which you stand; 
Recognize that everything which elevates the hero, 
Degrades the human. — 
But how — does not an equally gruesome demon, 
As that of war, 
Swing its black wing 
Over unhappy cities 
In times of peace? 
Yes — to the torment of humanity, 
It forms unfeeling jurists, <term>Jurists (Juristen)</term>: Legal experts or lawyers. The author is suggesting that the cold, detached application of law can be as destructive to the human spirit as the violence of war.

<summary>The author warns warriors that military glory is a deceptive "fairy tale" that blinds them to their own humanity, only becoming clear in the moment of death. He then pivots to argue that "unfeeling jurists" represent a demonic force in peacetime equal to the destruction of war.</summary>

<keywords>military glory, vanity, reason vs. armor, dehumanization, critique of law, peacetime demons, mortality</keywords>

--- Page 65 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's critique of society, moving from the "unfeeling jurists" introduced at the end of the previous page to a vivid description of legal corruption and the callousness of the wealthy. It describes how "chicanery" is forged in hell to destroy the lives of the innocent and humble.</meta>

And in the most secret dwelling of condemned spirits,
Where eternal night and darkness reign,
Cunning and <term>Chicanery (Chikane)</term> <note>In this context, "chicanery" refers to the use of legal trickery or petty technicalities to harass an opponent or delay justice.</note>
Are forged upon Satan's anvil.
Cunning destroys your humble house,
And your industrious threshold
Is swallowed up by chicanery.
In vain you stretch your hands
Up toward heaven.
Quarrels crowd out the voice of innocence
In the "lawful" noise
Before the blinded judge. <note>The judge is "blinded" not by impartiality, but likely by the "glittering dust" of bribes mentioned later.</note>
Bribed servants of gold undermine
With shameful tricks
The path upon which the honest man
Believes he walks in safety.
The cleverest of fraudsters scatter glittering dust
Into the eyes of Truth.
In vain the oppressed orphan
Calls upon the assistance of <term>Themis</term>. <note>Themis was the ancient Greek Titaness of divine law and order, traditionally depicted with the scales of justice.</note>
The verdict often blows away,
Like the soap bubble of a playing boy
When it leaves the creative straw
And scatters into a thousand tiny particles.
In this way, a black demon covers
The hearts of many rich men with triple bronze, <note>original: "dreyfachem Erze." This is a classical poetic metaphor—often used by Roman poets like Horace—to describe something impenetrable, hard, or unyielding.</note>
And sets them down, unfeeling, in marble palaces.

<summary>This page illustrates the corruption of the legal system, where deceit and "chicanery" forged in hell overwhelm the innocent and the poor. It concludes by describing how wealth and legal manipulation harden the hearts of the rich, making them as cold as their marble palaces.</summary>
<keywords>legal corruption, chicanery, injustice, Satan's anvil, Themis, greed, social critique, allegory</keywords>

There they fatten their bellies with the sighs of the poor,

No one hungers anymore.

--- Page 67 ---
<meta>This page continues the speaker's direct address to the wealthy and arrogant elite. Following the previous page’s imagery of the sun shining on a "lowly hut," the text now argues that the sun serves as a universal reminder of human equality, regardless of social status, and mourns the spiritual decay caused by selfishness.</meta>

Splendidly, just as it shines upon palaces,
To remind you,
That the lowliest of slaves <note>original: "Sklaven." In the 18th-century context, this refers to those in a state of total servitude or bondage.</note>
Is a human — just like you.
But in vain does my mouth speak of feeling.
Your soulless breast
No longer receives the gentle impression
Of innocent joys.
Your soul resembles those rivers
That are swollen by impure streams.
In vain does one seek the shining image
Of the azure sky within them.
Murky are the floods, and mud
Darkens the distorted mirror,
In which the Almighty <note>original: "Allmacht." Literally "Omnipotence," a common Enlightenment term for God as the supreme power.</note> once beheld Himself.
Humans are always armed against humans;
Equally mortal beings
Persecute one another unto death,
And kill even while dying.
Self-interest <note>original: "Eigennutz." The act of seeking one's own advantage at the expense of others.</note> always nourishes foolish pride
Within their animal breast.
"I am better than others":
Is the language of arrogance, <note>original: "Hochmuth." A specific type of overbearing pride or hubris often condemned in moral literature.</note>
And from this comes that spirit of persecution,
That wretched rage,
With an envious eye to always search
For faults in the heart of one's neighbor.

<note>Vocabulary used on this page: slaves, Almighty (God), self-interest, arrogance, persecution.</note>

<summary>The author asserts that the sun's universal light proves the common humanity of slaves and masters alike, while lamenting how arrogance and self-interest have corrupted the human soul. --- Page 68 ---
<meta>This page continues the moral reflection from the previous page, shifting from the general corruption of the soul to a specific critique of how society reacts to the misfortune of others. It introduces "Silvan" as a personification of the lost virtues of empathy and kindness.</meta>

When misfortune threatens a brother;
Or when from thundering clouds
The lightning bolt <note>original: "Wetterstral." An archaic term for a strike of lightning.</note> descends,
And sets his barns ablaze;
Then no tear wets the eye.
Misanthropic slander <note>original: "Schmähsucht." This refers to a malicious or compulsive urge to defame and mock others.</note>
Attributes vices to the unfortunate soul,
And our heart tells us:
He deserved it—it was punishment;
And we are often ten times
More punishable. — —
Oh! You did not think so, philanthropic Silvan; <note>The name Silvan likely refers to a deceased friend of the author or a literary figure representing a "friend of humanity."</note>
Your lips were always the protectors
Of human misery;
Never did you weigh down an unhappy brother
With new burdens;
Never did you sprinkle wormwood <note>original: "Wermuth." A proverbially bitter plant, used here metaphorically to describe adding bitterness or cruelty to someone's existing pain.</note> with malice
Into the cup of misfortune.
Alas! If you were still living—
But no—it is well for you that you died.
Your heart, O Silvan! How it would bleed,
To see daily so many living creatures
With human faces,
And to find so few
Who are truly human!

---

<note>Vocabulary used on this page: misfortune, slander, Silvan, misery, wormwood, creatures.</note>

<summary>The text laments the cruelty of people who blame the victim for their misfortunes and contrasts this with the character of the deceased Silvan, who practiced true empathy. It concludes with a poignant observation on the rarity of genuine humanity despite the abundance of human beings.</summary>

## The Countryside and the City.

### A Picture. <note>original: "Ein Gemälde." In 18th and 19th-century literature, a "painting" or "picture" was a specific genre of descriptive prose or poetry intended to evoke a visual scene in the reader's mind, often used for moral or emotional reflection.</note>

There in the desolate valley,
Where the lonely hut
Stands abandoned:
There where the spruce tree fearfully <note>original: "fürchterlich." While often translated as "terribly," in this poetic context, it suggests an awe-inspiring or precarious leaning over the heights.</note> leans
Over abysses, and rushing floods
With a terrible roar
Foaming over rocks
Plunge:
There sits by a majestic oak,
Which counts centuries,
At the foot of the dark mountains
A poor child.
Forsaken by all people,
This innocent creature plays
By the brook with stones,
And builds in the sand on the banks of the brooks
With innocent hands
Small mountains.
Toying, it experiences with delight
The days of childhood.
Ever new objects of nature
Entice its eager gaze.

# The Country and the City.

## A Portrait. <note>original: "Ein Gemälde." Literally a "painting," but used here to describe a literary sketch or vivid scene.</note>

---

There in the desolate valley,
Where the lonely hut
Stands abandoned:
There where the spruce tree fearfully leans
Over abysses, and rushing floods
With a terrible roar
Foaming over rocks
Plunge:
There sits by a majestic oak,
Which counts the centuries,
At the foot of the dark mountains
A poor child.
Abandoned by all people,
This innocent creature plays
By the brook with stones,
And builds on the edge of the brook's bank
With innocent hands
Little mountains.
Trifling away <note>original: "Tändelnd." Refers to playing idly or passing time in a lighthearted, carefree, childlike manner.</note> its time, it experiences with delight
The days of childhood.
Always new objects of nature
Entice its eager gaze.

<summary>This page introduces a new poetic section that contrasts the city and the country, beginning with a scene of an innocent child playing alone amidst a wild, majestic mountain landscape. The imagery emphasizes the raw power of nature—plunging floods and ancient oaks—against the simple, joyful play of childhood.</summary>

--- Page 72 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Portrait" of rural life established on page 47. Soon it admires the hundred suns,
Which shine in the brooks; <note>The "hundred suns" refers to the sunlight reflecting and fragmenting on the moving surface of the water.</note>
Soon its eager eye hastens
After the silvery scales
Of the playing fish.
Now it rushes after the butterflies,
Which with thousand-colored wings
Flutter on the flowers in the valley.
Now it catches one of the most beautiful
Among the summer-birds, <note>original: "Sommervögeln." An archaic German term for butterflies, though the child later refers to it simply as a "bird."</note>
And what can equal its joy?
Already it rushes toward its father,
Who, with sweat-covered face
At the setting sun,
Weary from farming,
Yearns for the arms
Of his wife.
"Here, Father!" cries the boy, "here, look,
What a beautiful bird
Have I caught for you! <note>The construction "Hab’ ich euch nicht gefangen!" is an idiomatic exclamation of pride, similar to "Look at this bird I've gone and caught!"</note>
Now he shall always live with us,
In the evening I will share my bread
With him,
And through the night he shall sleep
With me in my bed.
Of moss and flowers I will [prepare] for him,
The beautiful bird—"

Oh, if only I could always renew this image,
Which I once saw, within my soul!
Oh, how powerfully I still feel
The impression in my heart! —
Man of Nature! — innocent and pure like her,

--- Page 75 ---
<meta>This page continues the speaker's philosophical reflection on the "Man of Nature." Having previously described the healthy family unit and the physical vigor of rural inhabitants, the speaker now contrasts this natural simplicity with the artifice and corruption of city life.</meta>

And his eagle eye <note>original: "Adleraug." In 18th-century poetry, an "eagle eye" symbolized keen perception, vitality, and a soul unclouded by the "mists" of city vice.</note>—and the redness of the mother’s cheeks—tell us that innocence and health reside upon the soil where we live.
O happy people! Your enjoyment is the wealth of nature. Humanity knows no other treasures, no other happiness.
O brothers! Your virtue deserves that immutable mountains protect you from those people who inhabit the cities and poison the surrounding land.
You have little, brothers! But you are rich, for he is rich who has few needs.
Ah, if only I could live with you! I would gladly throw off these rags <note>The speaker uses "rags" (Fetzen) ironically to describe expensive, fashionable city clothing, suggesting that high-society fashion is spiritually worthless.</note> upon which the tailor sews my reputation.
Borders heavy with silk <note>original: "Bräme." This refers to the ornamental trimmings, furs, or edgings on formal garments that denoted social rank.</note>, paid for with gold, constitute a man’s worth here.
If you are not dressed up like a physician’s ape <note>original: "Aff des Arztes." A satirical reference to people who wear overly elaborate or ridiculous costumes to maintain a professional or social facade, much like a performing animal.</note>, every servant will mock you and turn you away from the doors from a distance.
Disfigure your face; smile when you wish to cry,

<summary>The author concludes the praise of rural life by contrasting the "wealth" of few needs with the "poverty" of urban artifice. He criticizes city society for valuing silk and status over character and for requiring people to mask their true emotions.</summary>

--- Page 76 ---
<meta>This page continues the speaker's critique of urban artificiality. Having previously praised the simple, honest life of the rural "Man of Nature," the speaker now details the exhausting rituals and physical discomforts required to maintain a fashionable appearance in the city.</meta>

Bow down, and crawl—this is the language of the 
->City.<-

Early in the morning I must already
Have my hair curled;
Following the twists and turns that Paris invented,
My locks must nestle,
And like a fog, flour-dust flies,
And travels over unpowdered heads. <note>original: "Mehlstaub." In the 18th century, fashionable wigs and hair were powdered with finely ground starch or wheat flour.</note>
So I must, I a creature of God,
Marred like a fool, act according to fashion.
O, if only custom had not given the right
To these follies,
What would people think,
If they saw people looking so?
Then I must gird my peaceful loins
Daily with long swords out of fashion,
And drag long rapiers, condemned to eternal peace,
Upon my hips. <note>original: "Degen." Smallswords or rapiers were an essential part of a gentleman's dress; they were symbols of social status rather than functional weapons.</note>
Then I must carry hats, useless for covering,
In my hands, <note>It was common for gentlemen to carry their hats under their arms (known as a "chapeau-bras") to avoid ruining their highly styled and powdered hair.</note>
And bow mechanically like an automaton,
And speak like a parrot:
Good morning! Good night! How do you 
->do?<-

Without sensation, without feeling
The one questioned answers me:
Quite well,—and your health? —

<summary>The speaker satirizes the absurd requirements of 18th-century urban fashion, from powdered hair and French styles to the wearing of useless swords. He laments how these social "follies" turn human beings into mindless, mechanical automatons.</summary>

--- Page 77 ---
<meta>This page continues the speaker's critique of the artificiality and moral decay found in urban high society. Having described the physical discomforts of fashion on the previous page, the speaker now focuses on the social and institutional corruption of the city.</meta>

And all of this as mechanically,
As a machine,
Only ever can be.
Then comes the time when, all dressed up,
In the antechambers of the wealthy
I hear the most dreadful of lies
That flatterers tell.
There come starving poets
With poems an ell long, <note>An "ell" (Elle) was a historical unit of length, roughly the length of a man’s arm, implying the poems are tediously long.</note>
And transform many a satyr into a Jupiter. <note>The speaker mocks how poets flatter their patrons; a "satyr" is a beastly, lustful creature of myth, while "Jupiter" is the majestic king of the gods.</note>
The hour of more serious business
Finally approaches, and in carriages
And sedan chairs <note>original: "Senften." These were windowed cabins carried on poles by two men, used by the wealthy to navigate crowded city streets without walking.</note>
Justice and Finance <note>The speaker personifies the legal and fiscal arms of the state.</note>
Hurry to their destination,
Pursued by a crowd
Of wailing people.
Tears of misery
From the poorest of litigants
Wash the blood from the Temple of Themis, <note>Themis was the ancient Greek goddess of divine law and order. Her "temple" represents the halls of justice.</note>
Which still clings to her threshold,
From slaughtered men.
Fat-fattened men,
Devoid of feeling, devoid of sensation,
Prove from authors weighing a hundredweight, <note>original: "zentnerschweren Autoren." A "Zentner" is a heavy unit of weight (approx. 50kg/110lbs). The speaker is mocking the massive, dense legal tomes used by bureaucrats to justify their lack of empathy.</note>
That gold weighs more
Than the wing of a gnat.

<term>Satyr</term> (mythological beast), <term>Jupiter</term> (king of gods), <term>Themis</term> (goddess of justice), <term>Justice</term> (Justiz), <term>Finance</term> (Finanz), <term>Carriages</term> (Karossen), <term>Sedan chairs</term> (Senften)

--- Page 78 ---
<meta>Continuing the speaker’s descent into the city's corruption. After describing the cruelty of the legal system on the previous page, the focus shifts here to the deceptive nature of academic and professional jargon and the superficiality of university degrees.</meta>

The frightened countryman
Listens in amazement to the magic spells, <note>The "magic spells" are a metaphor for the complex legal or academic jargon used to confuse and exploit uneducated people.</note>
And pays with hard-earned money
For incomprehensible words. —
But hush! — What a horrific noise
Allures my eager eye to other scenes?
What is this here, and who are these men in masks,
Who, like raving fools,
Heap sentence upon sentence,
Screaming like vultures, and chasing one another 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;like crows? —
They are scholars, I am told,
They are Doctors.
Doctors? — and what is the meaning of this ridiculous sleeve, <note>The speaker mocks the wide, ornamental sleeves of academic gowns.</note>
And this square of felt
Upon their wrinkled brows? —
These are the signs of their degree. <note>original: "Gradus." Referring to an academic rank or step.</note>
I thank you! — now the riddle
Is perfectly solved for me,
And I no longer wonder
At the written folios <note>A "folio" (Foliant) is a very large book, typically containing scholarly or legal texts.</note> of fools,
When a sleeve and a square of felt
Turn a man into a wise one.
Just so, sinewy boys jump
Into ice-cold water in the open square,
And by ancient custom
Are graduated in the fountain as butchers. <note>original: "Mezgern gradirt." This refers to the "Metzgersprung" (Butcher’s Leap), a traditional German initiation rite where apprentice butchers would jump into a public fountain to celebrate the completion of their training. The speaker is sarcastically comparing an academic degree to a splash in a fountain.</note>

<summary>This page satirizes the pretension of the academic elite, arguing that their complex language and formal regalia are merely "masks" used to swindle the common people and hide a lack of true wisdom.</summary>
<keywords>academia, social satire, Metzgersprung, academic dress, legal jargon, class criticism, 18th-century education</keywords>

--- Page 79 ---
<meta>Continuing the satire from the previous page regarding the superficial markers of status, the speaker expands his critique from the specific academic "Doctors" to a broader social commentary on how clothing and titles mask the true nature of men. This page reflects the influence of Enlightenment thought, particularly the works of the English poet Alexander Pope.</meta>

O human folly! Wisdom and greatness
You look for in things where they do not exist.
Some boast in rags, others in silk,
(Says Pope) <note>Alexander Pope (1688–1744), the English poet famous for his satirical verse. These lines are a loose translation/adaptation of Pope's *An Essay on Man*: "Worth makes the man, and want of it the fellow; / The rest is all but leather or prunella."</note> the shoemaker in his leather apron,
And the doctor in his gown.
What is as different as a gown and a leather apron,
As a wise man and a fool;
And yet merit alone makes the man,
And virtue the wise one;
Everything else is but gown and leather apron.
What is fame? — An imaginary life,
Through the breath of others,
Which before death is not in our power. <note>The speaker suggests that a person's reputation depends entirely on the fickle opinions of the public while they are still alive.</note>
Folly is everything that does not make us better,
That does not benefit mankind.
But in our time
The helmet-smith makes the hero,
The gold-embroiderer makes the knight,
And the tailor makes the doctor.
Only the fool makes himself,
For he no longer wears a cap. <note>A reference to the traditional "fool’s cap" with bells. The speaker implies that in a society obsessed with appearances, real fools are no longer recognizable because they dress like everyone else.</note>
Pinned with titles, hung with medals,
What are you more than what men can give you,
And slaves of men? —
An imaginary highness
Creates the vertigo of our reason. <note>original: "Schwindel unſrer Vernunft." Referring to the dizzying loss of rational thought when one is blinded by pride or social status.</note>

<summary>This page presents a poetic critique of social vanity, arguing that true wisdom and merit are found in virtue rather than in the titles, medals, or clothing—such as the doctor's gown or the hero's armor—that society uses to distinguish people.</summary>
<keywords>folly, Alexander Pope, merit, virtue, social satire, vanity, status symbols, Enlightenment philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 80 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page's critique of titles and professional gowns, the author now turns his attention to the internal vice of pride. He shifts from satirizing social ranks to examining the psychological trap of vanity, using the classical image of "hellebore" for madness and a vivid metaphor of a tethered insect to illustrate the fragility of human ego.</meta>

Above all other vices, give to Pride
The hellebore. <note>Hellebore (German: *Niesewurz*) was a medicinal plant used in antiquity and the early modern period to treat madness and melancholy. By prescribing it for pride, the author suggests that extreme vanity is a form of insanity.</note>
When a man can no longer climb upward,
Then he goes downward.
Nero degrades himself below the beasts; his baseness
Is but his ambition in a state of despair. <note>The Roman Emperor Nero (r. 54–68 AD) was often used in Enlightenment literature as the ultimate example of a ruler whose unchecked ego led to monstrous depravity.</note>
Have you never seen, tied to a long thread,
The humming beetle,
With which, in the days of May,
The lively boy
Plays in the evening? —
See how mottled it is, — various spots
Are pasted upon its wings.
A terrible cap covers its head,
And a tiny-cut little saber
Arms its trembling foot.
To the mockery of youth,
It stands there in the armor of a hero,
Or in the black cloak of a doctor. — <note>original: "Doktors." This refers to the academic or professional gown discussed on the previous page. The beetle is being "dressed up" by the child, just as humans dress up in titles and uniforms.</note>
We are this beetle, destined to live but a single month:
The boy is our childish pride,
Which robs us of the freedom of our destiny,
And with little spots shapes us
Into foolish figures.

<vocab>Pride, Hellebore, Nero, Ambition, Beetle, Freedom, Destiny</vocab>

<summary>This page uses a poignant metaphor of a child playing with a tethered beetle to illustrate how pride and vanity turn human beings into mocked, decorative playthings. It frames pride as a madness that restricts human freedom and reduces our potential to a mere "foolish figure."</summary>
<keywords>pride, hellebore, Nero, beetle metaphor, vanity, human nature, Enlightenment satire, social critique</keywords>

<meta>The author continues his critique of human vanity by contrasting it with the natural world. Collectively, they work
For the good of the whole.
No idle fool <note>original: "müßiger Thor." In 18th-century German, a "Thor" (modern: *Tor*) was not just a simpleton, but someone lacking the wisdom to live a productive, moral life.</note> feeds himself here
At the expense of others:
Everything is busy toward the goal,
And active for the whole.
With disgust my eye turns
From the constitution of the ant

In the days of spring
One sleeps in heating feathers <note>Refers to heavy feather-filled mattresses and duvets, which the author views as a symbol of decadence and sloth that distances humans from the natural morning.</note>

Letting the most beautiful morning pass by.
Finally she withdraws her steaming foot—
The lady—from the silk coverlet:
Slowly she goes to the waiting dressing table, <note>original: *Nachttisch*. While this translates literally as "nightstand," in this historical context it refers to the *toilette* or vanity table where a noblewoman's morning preparations—a public social event—took place.</note>

And philosophize and smile;
And Rome's holy geese <note>A reference to the sacred geese of the Temple of Juno on the Capitoline Hill, which, according to legend, saved Rome from a Gallic invasion in 390 BC by cackling and waking the defenders. Themselves give way with deepest reverence
To the cackle of the women,

The author uses the natural order to satirize the "holy" noise of social gossip and the waste of the morning hours.</summary>

# Felix, or the Freethinker. <note>The term "Freethinker" (German: *Freigeist*) was commonly used in the 18th and 19th centuries to describe individuals who rejected religious dogma and traditional morality in favor of reason, often used by religious writers to denote a "lost soul" or atheist.</note>

Felix was the joy of his parents,
While he was still a boy.
The father looked with longing
At the blossoming youth, <note>The term used is *Jüngling*, referring to the stage of life between adolescence and adulthood, a period contemporary moralists considered the most dangerous for one's character.</note>
Just as a gardener sees the flower
Which, faithful to his care,
Smiles back to the joy of his eye.
Often the good old man sat in the shady arbor,
And prayed from a pious heart to God
For blessings upon his children.
But the good father, how he was deceived,
When Felix entered the years of young adulthood!
Soon he faithlessly forgot
The lessons of wisdom
Which flowed from the lips of his father.
Like a spirited steed <note>original: "muthiges Roß." A classical metaphor comparing human passions to a powerful, unbridled horse that requires the "reins" of reason or religion.</note> he surrendered himself entirely
To his passions;
Seduced by wicked companions,
He dared to mock everything
That is sacred.
Virtue and religion were objects
Of his laughter.
In vain his father grieved himself away,

Oh son — soon the years of youth will be over.

Were these:
O youth, trust not him,
Who fears not God, and boldly
Mocks the laws of faith.

# On the Death
## of an Unfortunate Girl.

She is no more — her curly hair
No longer rolls down upon her beautiful bosom;
No zephyr <note>original: "Zephir." In Greek mythology, Zephyrus was the god of the west wind, often representing a gentle, warm breeze in poetry.</note> plays with her blonde curls anymore. — —
She is gone — the noble one! <note>original: "die Redliche." This term implies a person of integrity, honesty, and moral uprightness.</note> —
Powerfully she broke the structure
Of the burdensome dungeon, <note>A common 18th-century poetic metaphor viewing the physical body as a "prison" or "dungeon" (Kerker) that restricts the immortal soul.</note>
In which her immortal soul lay shackled.
Tired of the misery of life
She sought rest in a more blessed region. — —
Hier liegst du zerbrochen, schwächliche Hülle! —
Beautiful you once were, like a magnificent temple,
Because the image of the deity,
The soul, still inhabited you. — — —

But now — the eye shudders,
And horror thrones among the shattered
Ruins
Of beauty. — —

<summary>This page contains a mournful poem about the death of a young woman, implying she took her own life to escape earthly suffering. It utilizes the Enlightenment-era metaphor of the body as a temporary "dungeon" for the divine soul.</summary>
<keywords>mourning, soul, mortality, suicide, beauty, body-as-prison, 18th-century poetry</keywords>

--- Page 91 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page, where the narrator mourned over the "shattered ruins" of a beautiful woman who appears to have taken her own life, the text now shifts to the public's reaction. The previous page ended with a contemplation of her physical remains; this page addresses the moral debate surrounding her "undertaking" and "decision."</meta>

Here the eye of the mob <note>original: "des Pöbels." In this period, this term referred to the common masses or "rabble," often portrayed by writers as being unrefined or prone to harsh judgment.</note> lingers, and its
Blaspheming tongue sends hostile curses
After you into eternity.
Here one defends your undertaking,
There one slanders your decision.
Who is right — who is wrong? —

Not the spirit of fanaticism <note>original: "Schwärmerey." In the 18th century, this term was used pejoratively by Enlightenment thinkers to describe emotional or religious delusion and irrational enthusiasm.</note> shall spread its
->wing<-
Over me, and neither shall
Misanthropic vitriol
Howl curses into my ears. —

Let the spirit of the Christian glide with me
Over your corpse;
With the tear of gentleness <note>original: "Sanftmuth." A core 18th-century virtue combining meekness, compassion, and a soft-hearted nature.</note> in my eye
I will sing of you, unfortunate girl! —

Is the slave to be condemned, says the
->fanatic,<-
Who is weary of his life?
Is the slave to be condemned who, in the
->dungeon’s<-
Dark vaults,
Only more powerfully frees himself from the shackles, and with
Undaunted courage breaks through the marble
That locks away his freedom? —

<term>Pöbel</term>: mob or commoners; <term>Schwärmerey</term>: fanaticism or irrational enthusiasm; <term>Sanftmuth</term>: gentleness or meekness; <term>Sklave</term>: slave; <term>Kerkers</term>: dungeon or prison; <term>Freyheit</term>: freedom.

<summary>This page explores the conflicting public reactions to the young woman's suicide, contrasting the "mob's" curses with the narrator's compassionate "Christian" perspective. Who is stronger? Surely the slave at the oar!

--- Page 93 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded the "fanatic's" argument, which framed suicide as a brave escape from the "galley" of life. This page marks a shift in the poem’s dialogue, introducing the "Christian" perspective. It refutes the idea that suicide is a sign of strength, arguing instead that true fortitude lies in enduring suffering through faith. It also criticizes harsh religious zealots who condemn the suicidal, reminding them that God is a being of love and mercy, not just vengeance.</meta>

The guardian spirit of suffering humanity
Turns its startled gaze
Away from the boldest of transgressors, <note>original: "Frevler." In this context, it refers to the person who commits suicide, viewed here as one who violates the natural or divine order.</note>
And leads him through religion
To purer concepts. —
Not he, says the Christian, who the chain of des-
->tiny tears asunder,<-
Possesses manly strength. <note>original: "männliche Stärke." In the 18th century, "manly" virtue was often equated with Stoic endurance and the ability to withstand hardship without breaking.</note> —
He, who with childlike reverence
Wears the fetters as a gift from the Deity,
With which the Eternal burdens him,
He is stronger than the one
Who sinks down in despondency. —
Strengthened by virtue and religion,
The pious man sails across the stormy sea —
And awaits the signal of the Eternal
To make his landing. —
But you also err, says the Christian,
You, who with fanatic wrath,
Curse the unfortunate ones
Who have transgressed. <note>original: "Die sich vergangen." A euphemistic way of referring to those who have committed suicide (literally "those who have overstepped" or "gone astray").</note> —
Your gall-filled heart always arms
The Deity with avenging thunder, —
Though the Deity is Love itself;
Do you not see the image of its goodness
On every blooming field,
On every smiling flower,

<summary>This page presents the Christian counter-argument to suicide, defining true strength as the patient endurance of suffering rather than a violent escape. It also rebukes judgmental religious zealots, emphasizing that God is defined by love and mercy rather than vengeance.</summary>

--- Page 94 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Christian's" response from the previous page. Pity them;
This is the language of Christian forbearance. <note>original: "Duldung." This term implies a patient, long-suffering tolerance and mercy toward the failings of others.</note>

Upon the wounded hearts
Of the weeping parents. —
For who among you can know
Who gave her the key to the grave? —
Who among you can judge the construction of her nerves, <note>In the 18th century, "nerves" were often cited as the physical cause of emotional and mental distress; the author suggests that her actions may have been driven by a physical or constitutional sensitivity beyond her control.</note>

Perhaps the burden of life lay heavy upon her
When she ventured into the arms
Of death.
Now she is unburdened of the weight of her body;
Perhaps her eye sees more clearly,
Her ear hears more finely
The voice of the Creator.
Who among you wishes to condemn her?
Who among you is free of crime? <note>A reference to John 8:7: "Let him who is without sin among you be the first to throw a stone."</note>

These sacred ashes. —
I will pity you, best of maidens,

--- Page 96 ---
<meta>This page begins a new section titled "Greed," marking a thematic shift from the previous page's mournful elegy. While the preceding text focused on individual tragedy and Christian mercy, this poem adopts a philosophical tone, contrasting the perceived innocence of "primitive" life with the corruption of European society—a common theme in 18th-century Enlightenment literature.</meta>

# Greed.

Far away from human regions
The immense sea separated
Those blessed shores,
Where even purer air,
Than that in Europe,
Innocent creatures breathed.
There was the <term>Wilde</term> <note>The "wild man" or "savage." This reflects the 18th-century philosophical concept of the "Noble Savage," an idealized figure who lives in harmony with nature, uncorrupted by civilization.</note>, and ran naked
Through the groves,
Which the <term>Gottheit</term> <note>Deity or Godhead.</note>
Assigned to him for a dwelling;
Never did steel
Arm his hand—to kill
His brother;
Never did greed stir up
His calmly flowing blood
Within his veins.
If ever an arrow,
Swifter than thought,
Left his drawn bow;
It pierced either only
Into the heart of a lioness,
Or through the scaly armor
Of the crocodile,
To protect the <term>Naturmenschen</term> <note>Literally "nature-human" or "man of nature."</note>.

<vocab>Greed, Man of nature, Europe, Wild man, Deity, Crocodile</vocab>

<summary>This page introduces a poem about Greed, utilizing the "Noble Savage" trope to describe a prehistoric or distant paradise free from the violence and avarice of European civilization. It portrays a state of nature where weapons were used only for survival against wild animals, not for conflict between humans.</summary>
<keywords>Greed, Noble Savage, state of nature, Enlightenment philosophy, innocence, primitive life</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the poem "Greed" from the previous page. To his moderate palate
He never craved forbidden foods;
Gratefully he sat by the tree,

For his refreshment,

From a pure soul.
By chance his gaze turned
Toward the immeasurable plain
Of the sea,
And his wondering eye
Discovered floating machines
In the midst of the ocean waves;
Swaying, they came ever
Closer to the shore,
And grew to an enormous size.
Men, such as he was, but with whiter faces,

Tore the air in two, and a
thunder-like crack

->thren<-

<meta>This page continues the moral indictment of European colonialism begun on page 74. The previous page established the hospitality of the indigenous person and the betrayal by the Europeans. Say, why do you shackle
The free arms of innocence?
Where is your right? —
Never did the savage <note>original: "Wilde." The author uses this term ironically to contrast the peaceful indigenous person with the "barbaric" behavior of the Europeans.</note> devastate a friendly hearth <note>original: "Heerd." In the region
Where you lived.

dwelling,
And never did blood cling to his hands.

Ever killed your children?
Was he not content with the simple fruit
of his trees?
And yet he could not, in his wilderness, protect himself
Against you — you monsters! <note>original: "Unmenschen." Literally "un-humans" or "dehumanized ones." The author suggests the Europeans have lost their humanity through their cruelty.</note>
Quietly he lived out his days,
And would still be living them in bliss,

You tear the poor man from his fatherland,
To plague his days forever;

Which land the unfortunate soul
Across the foaming sea upon shores disgraced by vice. — <note>original: "Laster." Refers to the moral corruption and wickedness the author associates with the colonial slave-holding societies.</note>
My whole blood revolts at this thought.

<summary>This page presents a searing critique of the slave trade, arguing that the indigenous peoples lived in peace and innocence until the "monstrous" greed of European "barbarians" forced them into chains. The author concludes by expressing a visceral, physical revulsion at the injustice of slavery.</summary>

--- Page 101 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the previous page's condemnation of colonial greed and the "wicked" who bring destruction to a celebration of its moral opposite: the self-sacrificing hero. While the previous page lamented the lack of divine intervention against evildoers, this page presents Duke Leopold as an instrument of divine philanthropy who gave his life for others.</meta>

## On the Death
## Of Duke Leopold of Brunswick.

Not in the murderous battle,
Where the human blood of strangled brothers
Reddens the earth,
Fell the warrior of whom I sing.
His arm did not draw the sword
Against the crown of his human opponent,
And fear and death did not accompany the steps
Of the hero.
Divine philanthropy <note>original: "Philantropie." In the Enlightenment context, this refers specifically to "the love of mankind" as a practical, moral duty.</note> was at the side of the prince,
When he, at the altar
Of the love of mankind, fell,
And stretched out his arm not for the destruction of humanity,
But rather for the rescue of his unfortunate brother <note>This refers to the common citizens Duke Leopold was attempting to save from drowning during a flood.</note>.
No hero ever died so beautifully, and since the times of Codrus <note>Codrus was a legendary King of Athens who sacrificed himself to save his people; he was a popular historical comparison for self-sacrifice in the 18th century.</note>
Grateful posterity has gathered
No nobler ashes
Into its urns. —
Far and wide, the breaking waves
Of the turbulent Oder <note>The Oder River in Frankfurt (Oder). In April 1785, during a severe flood, Duke Leopold of Brunswick-Wolfenbüttel drowned while attempting to reach stranded villagers by boat.</note>
Spread destruction.

<note>Vocabulary: Duke Leopold of Brunswick, Philanthropy, Codrus, Oder River, Love of mankind</note>

<summary>This page contains a commemorative poem for Duke Leopold of Brunswick, who died in 1785 during a rescue attempt in a flood. The author contrasts his "noble" death for humanity with the violent, destructive deaths found on traditional battlefields.</summary>
<keywords>Duke Leopold of Brunswick, self-sacrifice, Enlightenment, humanitarianism, Codrus, Oder River flood, heroism, philanthropy</keywords>

--- Page 102 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the devastating flood of the Oder River in 1785, personifying the river as a destructive force seeking "freedom" from its banks. The boundaries that once marked her course,
She broke asunder, foaming,
And sought, to the terror of the fleeing citizen,
Unrestricted freedom. <note>The poet personifies the river as a wild entity that views its banks as shackles or "boundaries" to be violently cast off.</note>
Already the flood covered the countryside,
And bleating herds
Hurried toward the villages,
Pursued by the raging water.
With anxious care, the timid mother
Fled with the infant at her breast
To the higher roof,
Seeking rescue;
But already the current burrowed through the swaying hut,
And fled with the stolen loot
Of the trembling shepherd’s property.
Tears flow from the woman’s eye,
And in vain she calls for help;
The washed-out hut collapses,
And faithlessly, like a venal traitress, <note>original: "feile Verrätherinn." The poet suggests the house "sold out" its inhabitant by collapsing despite being her only place of refuge.</note>
Surrenders the mother to the water,
She who nonetheless sought refuge within it.
Buried beneath the floods,
The wailing cry of misery turns
Into a dull murmur.
Here, exhausted, the faithful hand of the rowing
Husband sinks,
