--- Page 104 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of Duke Leopold of Brunswick-Wolfenbüttel’s heroic response to the catastrophic Oder River flood of 1785. Following the previous page’s description of the sun rising over a landscape of death, the text now focuses on Leopold’s personal decision to intervene, culminating in his tragic drowning.</meta>

Already he boarded the boat; winged by love for humanity <note>original: "Menschenliebe." This term was a cornerstone of Enlightenment ethics, suggesting a rational and active compassion for all people regardless of rank.</note>
He hurries forth to save his brothers;
No other impulse reigns in his throbbing heart,
Than that of feeling for those who suffer.
The instinct of self-preservation dared in vain
To put a stop to his undertaking;
In vain its voice echoed
In Leopold’s soul, which heard no language
Other than the whimpering of the unfortunate.
Susceptible only to the danger of others,
He forgets his own,
Hurries—and sinks as a sacrifice of love. <note>Duke Leopold drowned on April 27, 1785, when his boat overturned while he was attempting to reach villagers stranded by the floodwaters of the Oder. His death was mourned across Europe as the ultimate act of "philanthropy."</note>
— Stay your course, boldest of floods,
Stop, and spare the friend of humanity! <note>original: "Menschenfreund." This was a common title given to Leopold after his death, celebrating him as a "friend of man."</note>
Is there then no deity that commands you
To have reverence for virtue?
But who knows the decrees of the Eternal? <note>original: "Rathschlüsse des Ewigen." A poetic way of referring to the inscrutable plans or will of God.</note>
Who knows the manners of death,
Which Providence <note>original: "Vorsicht." In 18th-century German, "Vorsicht" often referred to Divine Providence—the protective care of God or nature.</note> has inscribed in the Book of Life
For mortals?
Leopold dies—immortalized in the hearts of every person
Who feels virtue and righteousness. —
In the armor of a hero, he dies the death of love,

<vocab>Leopold, love for humanity, sacrifice, virtue, righteousness, decrees</vocab>

<summary>This page captures the climax of the poem, where Duke Leopold ignores his own safety to rescue flood victims, only to be swept away by the current. The poet frames his death as a noble sacrifice dictated by a mysterious divine providence.</summary>
<keywords>Leopold of Brunswick-Wolfenbüttel, self-sacrifice, philanthropy, 1785 flood, Divine Providence, virtue, heroism</keywords>

--- Page 105 ---
<meta>This page concludes the poetic comparison between the "false hero" (the warmonger) and the "true hero" (Duke Leopold). It continues the theme of Leopold's death as the ultimate expression of "Menschenliebe" (love for humanity) and civic virtue, contrasting his sacrifice with the destructive ambition of traditional conquerors.</meta>

And proves that he is not a hero,
Who to his mad greed and lust for plunder
Sacrifices hecatombs of people, <note>original: "Hekatomben." Originally referring to an ancient Greek sacrifice of one hundred oxen, here the term signifies a massive, senseless loss of human life in war.</note>
And for the spilled blood of hired men <note>original: "gemietheter Menschen." A reference to mercenary soldiers, reflecting the Enlightenment-era critique of rulers who treated soldiers as disposable property or commodities.</note>
Pays for follies that centuries mourn,
And then his lazy temples with laurel en-
->circles<- <note>The original German word "umgiebt" (to surround/encircle) is split across two lines, with the second half centered in the layout.</note>
Which his loyal citizens purchased,
And for which he himself struck no blow of the sword:
No, he does not deserve the name of hero,
Who so hideously disfigures humanity.
No—only he is a hero,
Who knows how to die,
To save people.

Auf <note>This catchword at the bottom of the page indicates that the next page begins with the word "Auf" (On/Upon).</note>

<summary>This page presents a poetic critique of traditional military "heroism," defining a true hero as one who sacrifices himself to save others rather than sacrificing subjects for personal glory. It concludes the tribute to Duke Leopold of Brunswick, who died during the 1785 Oder flood.</summary>

# On the Death of Kollmann,

->a scholar and spiritual counselor in Munich, who was<-
->shot at a summer pavilion.<- <note>original: "Lusthause." In the 18th century, a *Lusthaus* was a garden pavilion or small villa used for leisure and entertaining, not necessarily a place of ill repute.</note>

Amidst forms of a thousand kinds
The Angel of Death wanders
Around among mortals
As a straying traveler,
Enveloped in the garment of night.
He startles kings from purple beds,
And heroes tremble in armor at the sight
Of the strangler of men. <note>original: "Menschenwürgers." A dramatic personification of death as a violent executioner or predator.</note>

Now he comes in the flowering time of our years,
And snatches away the lively youth,
Who still with eager longing
Yearns for the enjoyment of ripening fruits.
Like the gale he snatches him away, who breaks
the budding rose
In its blossoms.
Now the busy man sinks in the summer of
his years
Beneath his devastating scythe,
Like the ear of grain, which under the hand of the reap-
er
Lowers its fruitful head to the earth.

Of unfeeling rich men.

<note>This passage uses vivid, macabre imagery—common in the "Graveyard Poetry" tradition—to personify the causes of death. It critiques social ills, suggesting that war-hungry kings, incompetent doctors, and the greed of the wealthy are the pillars that support the throne of Death.</note>

--- Page 117 ---
<meta>This page marks a shift from the previous content. While the preceding page concluded an epitaph for a deceased "Sage," this page begins a new celebratory poem dedicated to the return of Elector Carl Theodor to Munich. This reflects the common practice in 18th-century manuscripts of collecting "occasional poetry" written for specific civic or court events.</meta>

->### Upon the Return<-
->## of His Electoral Highness<-
-># **Carl Theodor**'s<-
->### to Munich.<-

---

How happy we are!
Blow over the bones of patriots, <note>In the 18th-century German context, "patriots" refers to citizens devoted to the welfare of their specific territory and loyal to their sovereign.</note>
Ye winds, softly,
And announce even to the dead the joy,
That Theodor is with us again!
O he is with us — he, whom we love! —
Joy dries the trembling tear
In the eye of the man,
Who, with gaze turned toward the earth,
Stood there melancholy, and cried out:
Theodor is no longer with us!
Despise him, Lyre, <note>The lyre is a classical symbol of the poet's art; the author is calling upon his music to shun anyone who lacks respect for the Elector.</note> whoever honors not this name,
And even if he stems from an ancient heroic lineage,
Despise him.
Happier days have snatched us again from sorrow, and
Given our Prince <note>original: "Fürsten," referring to Carl Theodor (1724–1799), the Prince-Elector who moved his court from Mannheim to Munich in 1778.</note> back to us.
O friends! how high the breast swells,
How high the heart's great feeling,
Unnamable sensation! ——

<vocab>Carl Theodor, Munich, Return, Patriots, Prince, Lyre, Heroic Lineage</vocab>

<summary>This page features a celebratory poem expressing public joy and devotion upon the return of Elector Carl Theodor to Munich. It uses high emotional language to contrast the sorrow of his absence with the current elation of the citizenry.</summary>
<keywords>Carl Theodor, Munich, Elector, patriotism, return, panegyric, Bavaria, 18th-century poetry</keywords>

--- Page 118 ---
<meta>This page continues the celebratory poem dedicated to Elector Carl Theodor. While the previous page focused on the immediate emotional response to his return to Munich, this page shifts to a broader, almost religious tone, framing the Elector as a paternal figure and the peace of his reign as a classical "Elysium." The catchword at the bottom indicates that the prayer to Providence continues on the following page.</meta>

O Happiness! Happiness! Not Elysium <note>In Greek mythology, Elysium (or the Elysian Fields) was the paradise reserved for the heroic and the virtuous. Here, the poet suggests the Elector's reign creates a heaven on earth.</note> alone
Knows who You are;
The happy citizens of the good Theodor
Know it as well,
For he too gave an Elysium to us.
Thanks to you, our father! <note>The "Landesvater" (Father of the Country) was a central concept in German absolutism, portraying the ruler as a stern but loving parent to his subjects.</note>
That we celebrate your festival and our own
Under the shadowing wings
Of a blessing-laden peace;
Not with the noisy splendor
Of a joy that only glimmers and sounds,
No—in a manner worthy of a Theodor,
With a deeply worshipful gaze toward the Ruler of the Worlds, <note>A reference to God as the ultimate sovereign over all creation.</note>
Who gave you to us as a father.
In quiet tranquility we celebrate your festival,
With joy in the heart, and in the eye
With tears.

---

Slumbering century!
Lift up your bowed head once more;
Slumbering fathers! Rise up,
And bless with us, and share with us
The holy joys,
Which Theodor gave back to us.
For happiness for him

<meta>This page transitions from the specific political patriotism of the previous section to a more universal, philosophical poem titled "Society." ## Society.

### A thought at the breaking of dawn.

You! — Artist of your own torments,
Poor mortal! — your bliss <note>The German "Seligkeit" can mean both heavenly salvation and earthly happiness; here it refers to the speaker's well-being within the community.</note>
Hangs upon the chains of love,
Which form the society of men.
Ah! Wretch! why do you seek
To tear these chains asunder,
And, out of the stubbornness
Of your unjust soul, accuse them
Of being a burden to humanity! —
Ah! how pitiable is the error
That deceives you!
Know, wretch! — you hasten yourself
Toward your own ruin! ——
Do you then fail to recognize the power of these bonds?
Through them, parts of the world are fettered,
And distant climes <note>Original: "Himmelsstriche," literally "sky-strokes," a common 18th-century term for climatic zones or geographical regions.</note> are united. ——
In vain are men separated
By the immeasurable partition of the sea;
Nothing remains upon the globe of the earth
That the chain of society does not link.
The whole world is now but a single land,
And the consequence of this divine harmony,

It warns the individual against the "pitiable error" of seeking total isolation or independence from society.</summary>

To pull humanity apart.

As treacherous as it is fearful,

In their place, humanity

# Noble Love.

Love! You noblest of feelings!
Softly you flow into the hearts of mortals.
Your warming spark
Creation has snatched from the holiest flame,
The flame that blazes only there,
There where the Godhead enthroned. <note>The "Godhead" (Gottheit) refers to the divine essence or God, suggesting that love is not merely a human emotion but a heavenly gift.</note>
Silently flows your electric current <note>The use of "electric current" reflects the late 18th and early 19th-century fascination with newly discovered scientific principles, viewing electricity as a vital, invisible force of life.</note>
Through the fibers of all creatures,
And spreads bliss, and magnetic power, <note>Like electricity, "magnetism" was a popular scientific metaphor for the invisible attraction and connection between human souls.</note>
And chains man to man,
And man to God.
It alone is the all-enlivening spirit,
The chain by which the Godhead
Bound worlds to worlds,
And suns to suns.
Softly the refreshing Zephyr <term>Zephyr: A classical literary term for a soft, gentle breeze, named after Zephyrus, the Greek god of the west wind.</term>
Kisses the balm of sweet scents
From flowers in the valley, and carries it away
To the shivering heights, where cheerful goats
Bleating, cling to the cliffs.
There, where boldness and courage
Drive the shepherd, to see the fairest girl
Of the mountainous region,

<meta>This page continues the poetic critique of human error initiated on the previous page. Falsehood raised its serpent’s head,

Extinguished the writing that in the Book of Nature was in-

know,
And for the most loathsome of vices
Self-interest built altars.
Mars was appointed the deity of murder,
And Jupiter, that most shameless scoundrel,
Who violated every virtuous girl,
To this Jupiter, Rome and Athens dedicated their tem-
ples.
To Venus, too, they built altars,
The goddess of the most shameful lust,
And incense they scattered to Priapus, <note>Priapus: A minor Greco-Roman god of fertility, frequently invoked in this era's literature as a symbol of unrestrained and "shameful" carnal excess.</note>

These were the gods of Rome,
The gods of the Spartans,
Until Socrates, the wisest of men,
Dared to wage war against these fantasies. <note>Original: "Abentheuer" (adventures/fables). The author uses this to dismiss the myths of the Greek gods as irrational stories rather than divine truth.</note>

The clamor of vice drowned her out,
And Socrates fell as a victim
At the altar of prejudice.
So it is with men; they persecute the one,

## To Miss von Baader
### in Munich,
#### as she was painting Ariadne on Naxos. <note>Ariadne on Naxos is a popular mythological subject in art, depicting the Cretan princess after she was abandoned by Theseus on the island of Naxos. Where have you perhaps borrowed such art,
You painter of souls! <note>original: "Seelenmalerinn." A term of high praise in the late 18th century for an artist who could capture the internal emotional state of a subject.</note> To represent feelings visibly,
And to chain the passions,
And to hold them fast in colors,
And to bind them through brushstrokes? —
Prometheus stole fire from the gods once
To bring humans to life:
You stole even more, you thief of the spirit!
Your brush can only come from Olympus;
It must be the brush with which Zeus once
Painted the plan of creation.
It is that very one; yet you did not steal this brush;
Zeus gave it to you — your eye penetrated deep into the sanctuary
Of Art — the sister of Nature,
From whom you learned to draw souls,
And to express passions,
And to embody spiritual forces,
You herald of psychology! <term>Seelenkunde: Literally "soul-knowledge." In this era, the term referred to the observation and study of the human psyche and character, often through the lens of philosophy and art rather than clinical science.</term>
Your painting is a book, for those who can read it,

You painter of souls! <note>original: "Seelenmalerinn." This is a feminine form of "soul-painter," a term of high praise in the late 18th century for an artist who could capture the internal emotional state of a subject.</note> To represent feelings visibly,
And to chain the passions,
And to hold them fast in colors,
And to bind them through brushstrokes? —
Prometheus stole fire from the gods once
To bring humans to life:
You stole even more, you thief of the spirit!
Your brush can only come from Olympus;
It must be the brush with which Zeus once
Painted the plan of creation.
It is that very one; yet you did not steal this brush;
Zeus gave it to you — your eye penetrated deep into the sanctuary
Of Art — the sister of Nature,
From whom you learned to draw souls,
And to express passions,
And to embody spiritual forces,
You herald of psychology! <term>Seelenkunde: Literally "soul-knowledge." In this era, the term referred to the observation and study of the human psyche and character, often through the lens of philosophy and art rather than clinical science.</term>
Your painting is a book, for those who can read it,

<summary>This page features a celebratory poem dedicated to Miss von Baader, an artist in Munich, praising her ability to capture the human psyche through her painting of the mythological figure Ariadne. The author uses classical Greek imagery, such as Prometheus and Zeus, to elevate the artist's skill to a divine level.</summary>

## On the Death of a Female Friend.

---

Who are you, you who rest here beneath the earth,
Withdrawn from the adversities of humankind? —
You hid yourself in the mother’s womb, <note>In this context, the poet likely refers to "Mother Earth," suggesting a return to the soil from which life springs.</note>
And established your dwelling place among the worms.

It is dark there in the grave,
The sunbeam does not penetrate into this depth;
My eye recoils at the sight of decay.

How ghastly is the devastation that reigns here!
Your eye is clouded, your cheek pale,
And corruption covers your harmonious features;
Ghastly is your sight;
But no! It is not you; it is your shell,
Which fell to ruin and paid the toll of materiality. <note>original: "Sinnlichkeit." While this often means "sensuality," here it refers to the physical, sensory world and the debt all mortal flesh owes to nature.</note>
The garment is cast aside,
That covered you in mortality.
Now you wear the bridal gown of light,
Which is no longer subject to any decay.
Why do I shudder,
When I see the decayed remains of a body,
Which, within the span of time, enclosed an immortal soul;
Even in the rubble, the ruins of prisons are ghastly. <note>The "prison" is a common Enlightenment-era metaphor for the physical body, which was thought to confine the infinite spirit until death provided liberation.</note>

<meta>catchword: Fashion</meta> <note>original: "Modes." This catchword suggests the following page may discuss "Modes" (Fashions) or social customs.</note>

--- Page 135 ---
<meta>This page continues the elegiac poem for a deceased female friend. While the previous page contemplated the physical reality of the grave, this page reflects on the philosophical and alchemical nature of death, contrasting the "dust" of the physical body with the immortal "calling" of the spirit.</meta>

Decay together, ruined hut! <note>A common poetic metaphor where the "hut" represents the physical body as a temporary, fragile dwelling for the soul.</note>
In which my friend languished so long,
Endured so much misery,
For life — human life is miserable,
Because it separates us from the home of the spirits,
And places us in the vale of tears; <note>The "Vale of Tears" (Latin: *valle lacrimarum*) is a Christian reference to the tribulations of earthly life compared to the joy of heaven.</note>
In vain one languishes here below for happiness,
The earth has no happiness —
Happiness is the portion of souls,
Created for higher dwellings.
Dust can give no happiness to spirits,
And even if the dust sits upon a throne,
And even if purple clothes the dust, <note>Purple cloth was a historical symbol of royalty and high status; the poet argues that even a king is merely "clothed dust."</note>
It remains dust, which one day decays,
Which winds one day blow away,
Dust like the dust of a worm.
It is a shell that covers us,
Composed of flesh and blood,
Created for suffering,
And abandoned to the play of the elements.
Fortunate is he who knows his calling,
And does not sink deeper into the filth of sensuality, <note>original: "Sinnlichkeit." This refers to the distractions of the physical, sensory world over the spiritual.</note>
And does not amalgamate himself with bodies. <note>The poet uses the alchemical term "amalgamate" to describe the soul becoming too tightly bound to material things.</note>
When the great master of separation comes—Death, <note>original: "Scheidekünstler." An alchemical term for a chemist or refiner who separates pure metals from dross.</note>
Unfortunate then is he who, in the time of the great
->Probe<- <note>original: "Probe." In alchemy, this refers to a trial or assay to test the purity of a substance.</note>
Does not withstand the cupel, <note>original: "Capelle." A cupel is a shallow, porous vessel used in alchemy and assaying to refine precious metals under intense heat. The poet suggests that death is the fire that tests the soul's purity.</note>

<summary>The poet reflects on the physical body as a decaying "hut" and "dust," contrasting earthly suffering with the soul's true home. Death is depicted as an alchemist who separates the spiritual essence from the material shell through a trial by fire.</summary>
<keywords>death, alchemy, soul, immortality, dust, Scheidekünstler, cupel, transience</keywords>

<meta>This page marks a transition from the preceding funeral elegy for a woman to a new poem addressed to Prince Frederick of Hesse-Darmstadt. While the previous text focused on domestic virtue and personal loss, this page shifts to a public address on the nature of "Wohlthätigkeit" (benevolence or charity) and critiques the coldness of urban and courtly life.</meta>

->To<-
### Prince Frederick of Hesse-Darmstadt.

Heavenly feeling, you who stream from the source of the
->**Divinity**,<-
Sweet inclination of noble and good souls,
Divine feeling of charity! <note>original: "Wohlthätigkeit." In the 18th century, this term implied more than just giving money; it described a deep, active compassion and moral duty toward the suffering of others.</note>
O how happy is the mortal
Who lives under your dominion!
How happy is he who consecrates his heart entirely to you!
Thrice blessed is the life of the nobleman
Who early on learns to recognize the false shimmer of this life,
->recognize,<-
And the deceitful cunning of hypocritical passions
That dishonor mankind.
O how blessed is he who, far from cities,
Dwells in a rural cottage,
And whose business it is to comfort suffering
->humanity!<-
Who can see the tears of the unfortunate
Without being moved, without feeling?
Those iron souls that only the city produces,
That only the false politics of the courts shape!
There, the heart is closed to all sympathy;
There, the sanctity of tears has no value.

<note>Prince Frederick of Hesse-Darmstadt (1759–1802) was a member of the ruling house of Hesse-Darmstadt. The poet addresses him here using the "Enlightened" ideal of a ruler: one who rejects the artifice of the court in favor of genuine emotion and humanitarian duty.</note>

--- Page 138 ---
<meta>This page continues the poem addressed to Prince Frederick of Hesse-Darmstadt. Following the previous page's critique of the coldness found in cities and royal courts, the text now focuses on the internal moral decay of the selfish individual compared to the spiritual rewards of the compassionate heart.</meta>

Every feeling of piety is stifled there,
And buried beneath vices.
Oh, how I pity the mortal
Who knows no other "I" but himself,
Who lives only for himself, acts only for himself!
His greedy heart is tortured day and night
By the drafting of intrigues;
His never-to-be-satisfied egoism <note>In the 18th century, "Egoismus" (egoism) was a relatively new term in German, used here to describe a soul entirely closed off from social duty and communal feeling.</note>
Thirsts constantly for gold, for honors,
Never did the sacred flame of philanthropy <note>original: "Menschenliebe." A central concept of the Enlightenment, this refers to a universal love for mankind that transcends social rank.</note> flare up within him;
He lives for no one but himself;
O unhappy man! And you deprive yourself of the noblest and
gentlest of pleasures,
Of the pleasure of giving and of making others happy.
Be blessed by me, benevolent Divinity!
You who gave me a heart receptive to the sufferings
of my brothers.
To be sure, many an hour is sad for the compassionate man;
His heart is also tortured by the suffering of others,
Having already been tortured enough by his own.
But the reward far exceeds all those sufferings.
The tear that flows from the eye of the grateful man,
The embrace of the good,
What compensation for past sorrows!

## My Wish.

If it were indeed granted to me
To choose among the gifts of the gods,
Then I would never have chosen gold, nor silver,
Never vain honor, never the splendor of the throne.
All this would be dust to me.
Reason and Spirit! You alone could have
->made me long for them,<-
But I no longer even crave you;
I see so many people unhappy because of you,
And what are you then, Reason <note>original: "Verstand." While it can mean "intellect," in this 18th-century context it refers to the cold, analytical rationality of the Enlightenment.</note>, without Feeling? <note>original: "Empfindung." This term is a key concept of the *Empfindsamkeit* (Sentimentalism) movement, suggesting that true humanity requires the ability to feel and empathize, not just to think.</note>
Mighty Deity! You who give life to all,
Grant me from your most magnificent gifts—
From your blessings—only a few.
Give me a feeling heart, a friend,
And sound common sense. <note>original: "gesunden Menschenverstand." This refers to practical wisdom and grounded judgment, reflecting the era's shift toward accessible, human-centric philosophy.</note>

<meta>This page follows a poem titled "My Wish," where the author rejected material wealth and cold intellect in favor of a "feeling heart" and common sense. Here, the author continues this journey of emotional and spiritual seeking by ascending a mountain, a common trope in 18th-century literature to represent a move away from the "artificial" world of society toward the "sublime" purity of nature.</meta>

# The Mountain.

Receive my greeting, majestic mountain,
Whose head is shrouded in clouds,
Receive my greeting!
For love of you I left the flowering field,
The brooks rippling in the valley,
The shade of the forests —
Everything I left, in order your high, lonely summit
->to climb.<-

Oh, how peaceful I am here!
Closer to the Godhead <note>original: "Gottheit." This reflects a Deist or Pantheistic view common in the late 18th century, where the divine is experienced through the grandeur of the natural world rather than strictly through church dogma.</note>, further removed from men,
Who in my eyes now swarm like ants.
Here I am safe for a time from their
->persecutions.<- <note>original: "Verfolgungen." While it can mean literal persecution, in the context of Sentimentalist poetry, it often refers to the social pressures, judgments, and emotional exhaustion caused by life in high society.</note>

From afar I survey all of nature;
She alone is still great,
All else, being the work of men,
Is small, and vanishes before my eyes.
Here I feel the purest impulse for adoration,
Nothing disturbs me; no sorrowful image of a sufferer
Draws my attention,
All that is called human misery is far beneath me,
Even the struggle of the elements. <note>original: "Kampf der Elemente." A reference to the chaotic forces of nature, such as storms; the poet feels so spiritually elevated that even the physical weather seems distant.</note>
I live in a pure sphere.

<meta>catchword: The</meta>

<summary>The author describes ascending a mountain to find spiritual peace and escape the "persecutions" of human society. The poem characterizes nature as the only true greatness, allowing the narrator to reach a state of pure adoration closer to the divine.</summary>
<keywords>Nature, Sublime, Godhead, Isolation, Humanity, Perspective, Mountains, Adoration</keywords>

The thunder rolls beneath me,
The lightning snakes beneath my feet,
Above me there is peace and all is serene.
My gaze encompasses entire regions,
I see the storms of the ocean,

->shudder,<- <note>original: "heiligen Schauer." A key concept in 18th-century aesthetics called the "Sublime"—a feeling of overwhelming awe, often mixed with a pleasant terror, when facing the vastness of nature or the divine.</note>

Where vice and falsehood sit enthroned!
With every step that I descend,
I feel that I am approaching the world again.
Truth covers itself again with its veil, <note>A common metaphor in Enlightenment and Romantic literature where "Truth" is a goddess or a light that becomes hidden by the "veil" of human social conventions and lies.</note>

The more I feel that error and darkness
->surround it.<-

<meta>Continuity: This page follows the narrator’s descent from a mountain summit. # The Machiavellian Courtier.

Most shameful of toads, you who crawl in the dust,
Devouring every unhealthy breath of the earth,
And in your hideous body
Boiling it into the most poisonous venom, <note>original: "Geifer." This refers to toxic saliva or foam, symbolizing the bitter, malicious speech of a courtier.</note>
Most shameful of beasts! lend me all your qualities,
And you, viper! your supple nature,
Your crawling manner,
And your art of entangling, of flicking your tongue, of
killing.
Lend me this, and then, tiger! <note>original: "Tyger." An archaic spelling common in both German and English in the 18th century.</note> give me
Your cruelty.
And you, wild boar! your insolence,
And I will mix all these ingredients
With the pride of an unruly steed,
With the cunning of the fox,
With the wantonness <note>original: "Ausgelassenheit." In this context, it refers to a lack of moral restraint or lewd behavior, traditionally associated with the he-goat.</note> of the he-goat,
With the baseness of the pig,
And the absurdity of the ape:
And when this mixture
In its total depravity

<note>The author uses a "bestiary" of vices—a common literary device where human failings are represented by animal traits. By invoking these animals, the narrator constructs a "recipe" for the perfect, corrupt courtier who serves only his own interests through Machiavellian schemes.</note>

<term>Machiavellianism</term>: Named after Niccolò Machiavelli, this term refers to political behavior characterized by cunning, duplicity, and a belief that "the end justifies the means." In 18th-century literature, the "Machiavellian Courtier" was a popular villainous archetype representing the corruption of the royal court.

<summary>Following his descent from the mountain, the narrator delivers a scathing vituperation against the "Machiavellian Courtier," personifying the vices of the court through a grotesque mixture of animal traits like the toad’s venom and the viper’s cunning.</summary>
<keywords>Machiavellianism, courtier, vice, allegory, toad, viper, tiger, boar, steed, fox, he-goat, ape, corruption</keywords>

--- Page 145 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page completes the thought from page 120. Having listed a "recipe" of animalistic vices—poison, cruelty, cunning, and absurdity—the author now describes the final result: the "fully formed" soul of a courtier.</meta>

Has attained the highest degree of moral rot, <note>original: "moralischen Fäulniß." A strong term suggesting that the corruption is not just surface-level but a deep internal decay.</note>
Then it stands there, fully formed,
The soul of the courtier,
The soul of the Machiavellian. <note>Referencing Niccolò Machiavelli, whose name became synonymous with political deception and the idea that the ends justify any means, no matter how immoral.</note>
When you have no name left for shaming,
When you have exhausted all ideas
To revile a man,
There remains for you the epitome of all titles of vice,
Which expresses more through one word
Than all the words expressed
With which one branded the shameful deeds from the first criminal
          to the last, — <note>original: "brandmarkte." This refers to the historical practice of literally burning a mark into a criminal's skin as a permanent sign of their infamy.</note>
The word — he is a courtier,
This says it all;
That is to say: He can smile,
Smile gently, and yet be a villain; <note>This closely mirrors a famous sentiment from Shakespeare's *Hamlet*: "That one may smile, and smile, and be a villain."</note>
That is to say: — He can joke and kill,
Flatter and slander,
Grovel and wound,
Have tears in his eyes and malice in his heart,
Praise and hate,
Elevate and overthrow.
This image is too weak
To paint the abominableness
Which characterizes the courtier.

<summary>This page concludes a scathing polemic against the "courtier," arguing that the title itself is the ultimate insult, encompassing every possible human vice and hypocrisy.</summary>

**Vocabulary:** Soul, courtier, specters, courts

<summary>This page concludes a scathing indictment of courtly life, suggesting that if a courtier's internal corruption were visible, it would be more terrifying than any ghost. ## My Sufferings.

O God! where shall I find rest and peace again,
->find,<- <note>The author uses "find" as a continuation of the previous line, emphasizing a search for tranquility that feels out of reach.</note>
For which my heart craves in many an hour;
Since bonds of steel tie me to court and cities,
Where no joy any longer stirs the wounded soul.
To the love of humanity was my gentle heart raised;
<note>original: "Menschenliebe." This term was a cornerstone of the Enlightenment, referring to a secular, humanitarian love for fellow humans regardless of rank.</note>
When people suffered, then my soul suffered
->too;<-
Never has my knee bowed cowardly before power and pride,
->bowed,<-
I never paid homage to whims, or to custom.
Never did my heart cringe at the shimmer of false
->greatness,<-
Human deceit was always hateful to my
->spirit,<-
Poverty was dear to me, I covered its nakedness,
And often took it into protection against fools in the pal-
->ace.<-
I dared with courage to tell those above,
That vice is their pleasure and their joy;
I was never deceived by the appearance applied in make-
->up,<- <note>original: "Schminke." In the 18th century, heavy lead-based makeup was standard at court, serving here as a metaphor for the literal and moral masks worn by the aristocracy.</note>
Before the false shimmer, my bright eye
->was never blind.<-

**Vocabulary:** Sufferings, Rest, Peace, Heart, Love of humanity, Soul, Poverty, Human deceit, Vice, Makeup

They present themselves as a person of Enlightenment principles who prioritizes empathy for the poor and truth-telling over the superficiality and vice of high society.</summary>

<meta>Continuity: This page continues the poem from page 124–125. While others spent their time in revelry, <note>original: "Schwärmerey." In the 18th century, this often referred to excessive, unguided enthusiasm or idle, flighty behavior—here contrasted with the author’s serious study.</note>
    Where many a youth sat with girls and wine,
I was at my desk, and wrote for you,
    And this eye was often wet with tears for you.

I felt your suffering, I felt your wound,
    The orphan's tear that flowed from his eye,
I took him in, and rejoiced in the hour,
    In which I poured soothing relief into his wound.

My small house was never closed to the poor man,
    I listened to everyone who came to me in fear,
And when the tears of sorrow flowed from his eye,
    Then it was surely my heart that felt a warm sympathy. <note>original: "Antheil." A central Enlightenment concept meaning more than just pity; it implies an active, shared feeling or "taking part" in another's fate.</note>

It was only sad for me when I found myself 
    Sometimes weaker in helping through deeds
Than my will desired;
    But in those cases, I always gave counsel,
    Just as one advises brothers in the face of looming danger.

Often under the pressure on humanity, amid political intrigues,
    Where people shrug their shoulders and know of no help,

J 2

All of this was hostile to me, and sought to harm me;
So much does it cost to be wise here on earth;
From the stern master to [His] Excellency and Grace,
They gladly spit in the face of the <term>thinking man</term> <note>original: "dem Denkenden." This refers to the Enlightenment ideal of the "philosophe" or independent thinker who prioritizes reason over dogma, often facing social ostracization for doing so.</note>.

->to live!<-

What can the noble man do for these people?
He may give his possessions and property and even his blood
->give,<-
They never show him gratitude, and never leave him in peace.
Their spirit is too low for true feelings of gratitude,
Only <term>malicious joy</term> <note>original: "Schadenfreude." The specific pleasure derived from the misfortune or humiliation of others, which the author identifies as the primary motivation of his detractors.</note> nourishes their completely corrupted heart;
Honor, good name, and reputation are but games to these people;
To dishonor, to be harmful, that is for them only a
->joke.<-

Their <term>intolerant spirit</term> <note>original: "duldungsloser Geist." A central Enlightenment grievance against those who refused to accept religious or philosophical pluralism.</note> is entirely without love,

Their tongue is like a sword; with repeated
->blows,<-
It severs the bond of humanity, the bond of
->love in two.<-

They would pile up—were it still advisable in this day and age—
Surely with <term>priestly rage</term> <note>original: "Bonzenwuth." "Bonze" was a derogatory term for powerful, arrogant, or hypocritical clergy or officials. The fool will envy talent, the scoundrel virtue,
So life becomes a burden to the <term>honest man</term> <note>original: "Redlichen." A person characterized by integrity, sincerity, and uprightness, often contrasted in this era with the "courtier" who relies on deception.</note>.
One never approaches a man directly; through secret intrigue
The <term>passion for cabals</term> <note>original: "Cabalensucht." A compulsive desire to engage in "cabals"—secret political factions or plots to overthrow or undermine others at court.</note> brings ruin here, and smiles all the while,
Regrets when it crushes, and rejoices in its victories,
And every thinking person becomes discouraged, timid, shy.
Who can, in this situation, in extreme ruin,
Be useful to the Fatherland and the <term>Sovereign</term> <note>original: "Fürsten." The ruling prince or head of state.</note>?

Only the thought of death can gladden the noble soul.
He foresees the future; it cannot get better,
For it is only virtue that preserves happiness and well-being,
And that is being chased away from this wide earth;
What wonder then, if the state and the salvation of nations fall.
With joy will I one day smile toward death,
Which takes me from the world out of this <term>dungeon’s vault</term> <note>original: "Kerkers Gruft." A powerful metaphor. The author views the corrupt social and political world as a prison, seeing physical death as the only true liberation for a virtuous spirit.</note>,
And I will rejoice at my final death-rattle,
That my hour calls me into better worlds.

<summary>This page concludes a poetic reflection on the toxic environment of the royal court, where intrigue and envy suppress virtue. The author expresses a profound sense of hopelessness for the state and finds comfort only in the prospect of death as an escape to a "better world."</summary>

others.

# L O V E .

---

Feeling of tenderness, you gift of beautiful souls! <note>original: "schöner Seelen." A reference to the 18th-century concept of the "Beautiful Soul" (Schöne Seele), a person whose outward actions and inner inclinations are in perfect moral harmony.</note>
->You heavenly gift, which the Deity gave us!<-
Let your beauty be placed before my eyes,
->And imprint your angelic image upon my heart.<-
Feeling of tenderness! You reflection of pure love!
->Who bestows joy of life, and gives life's bliss.<-
You, the archetype <note>original: "Typus." In this context, it refers to a manifestation or an earthly model that represents the divine nature.</note> of the Deity! You, the impulse of the purest impulses!
->How great our "Self" <note>original: "unser Ich." Literally "our I," referring to the ego or the essence of human identity.</note> becomes when our soul loves.<-

What are you, Love? Say, how many people know
->What it truly means to love; people flirt, pine, and kiss,<-
Embrace one another, look at each other, and in that tender longing
->Our heart feels that it misses something deep within.<-

O humanity! Do learn: the true source of love
->Lies not in the senses; it is a part of the spirit;<-
It is a gentle fire, a possession of the soul,
->And does not offer its treasure for sale to the senses alone.<-

<note>Vocabulary: Love, tenderness, deity, soul, joy of life, senses, part of the spirit.</note>

<summary>This page contains a poem defining love and tenderness as divine, spiritual qualities rather than physical ones. The author argues that true love elevates the human soul and originates in the spirit rather than the senses.</summary>
<keywords>Love, tenderness, beautiful soul, divinity, spirituality vs. sensuality, philosophy of emotion</keywords>

### Pocketbook for Princes.

---

Prince! fear a God who rules above you,
This is the first step that leads to wisdom. <note>original: "Weißheit." An archaic spelling of *Weisheit*.</note>
Love religion; be always the savior of virtue;
Remove from your court the vain mockers of faith.
He who forgets the fear of God knows no duty,
He who does not love his God does not love the Prince
either.
The free-thinker is a man who is arrogant in
himself, <term>Freygeist (Free-thinker): In the 18th and early 19th centuries, this term often carried a negative connotation, implying not just independent thought but an atheistic lack of moral restraint.</term>
Who knows nothing of faith, who has forgotten his God:
Who is without virtue, and without inner strength,
A slave and a servant to every passion,
Who swears loyalty to you today, and does not keep it
tomorrow,
Who oppresses his fellow man and manages his office
poorly,
Who boldly profanes everything that is still called holy,
Who drives the steel into the breast of his best friend,
When he wishes to kill: who acts without
principle,
And dares every vice whenever the desire strikes him.
Tell me, if a monster that so strives for vice,
Lives at your side, Prince! at your court,

<meta>catchword: What</meta>
<vocab>Fear of God, Wisdom, Free-thinker, Virtue, Vice, Prince</vocab>

<summary>This page addresses a Prince, arguing that the "fear of God" is the essential foundation for a ruler's wisdom and a subject's loyalty. It warns specifically against "free-thinkers," portraying them as untrustworthy, slave to their passions, and potentially murderous due to their lack of religious principles.</summary>
<keywords>prince, religion, fear of God, free-thinker, virtue, vice, loyalty, morality, political advice</keywords>

--- Page 169 ---
<meta>Continuity: Following the argument that "free-thinkers" are inherently untrustworthy and dangerous to a ruler's safety, this page continues the poetic exhortation to the Prince, contrasting the destabilizing influence of the godless with the loyalty of the virtuous Christian subject.</meta>

What misery threatens you yourself, and your whole states!
He who fears no God dares to betray princes.

Only he, my dear Prince! who possesses true virtue,
Only he, believe me, is a good man in the state.
He knows piety, and follows his duties faithfully;
To him, the Prince is always worthy; his fatherland always sacred.
Recognize this man by his fairness, <term>Billigkeit (Fairness/Equity): In this context, it refers to a sense of moral justice and propriety rather than just being "inexpensive."</term>
Free from hypocrisy in honest integrity. <term>Bieder (Honest/Upright): An archaic term for a person of solid, honorable character; it suggests a reliable and unpretentious morality.</term>
He who mocks virtue at your court, Prince!
Believe that he is evil, and gathers with the wicked. <note>original: "sich zum Bösen rottet." To "rotten" implies forming a mob or a conspiracy for malicious purposes.</note>
A good man is never ashamed to dedicate himself to virtue,
He is never ashamed, my Prince! to be a good Christian.
It has always been, Prince! the artifice of many great men,
To call matters of faith frivolous antics. <note>original: "eitle Possen." Meaning empty jokes or foolish nonsense.</note>
The reason is obvious: faith restrains us,
And malice wishes to be entirely unrestrained.
Loud is the contradiction of believing in a God,
And yet at the same time plundering lands at one's whim.
Not like an executioner does the Christian slay his neighbor:
He knows the value of humanity; his Gospel.

--- Page 170 ---

With humility he follows the sacred laws,
And he will never violate the duty of love for mankind;
He follows pure love. In this holy book
Nothing is written of robbery, of human deceit, and curses.
And yet, men still wish to murder, rob, and oppress;
Therefore, they must nonetheless patch together a little faith for themselves: <note>original: "sein Gläubchen flicken." A dismissive term suggesting that those who wish to sin try to "mend" or "tailor" their religious beliefs to suit their own convenience rather than following true doctrine.</note>

And if it cannot be patched well, then they madly and boldly
Trample the law into the ground with shamelessness,
Following only passion and the stimulation of their lusts,
As if the heart knew not a single syllable of God.
Faith, it is said, O Prince! is only made for the common people;
By the "Great ones" it has always been laughed at in their hearts.
Outwardly, one must appear to be something for the sake of the people;

But inwardly, a Prince may believe or think
Whatever he himself desires. These base men!
How miserable they would often like to see their princes!
The reason, my Prince, is very easy to fathom here:

Vice can only find protection among the vicious.
The wicked man knows it well: if princes are virtuous,
Then vice will soon be banished from their court.
Despise those, my Prince! who are ashamed of their faith,
For they are inclined toward every [shady] undertaking. <term>Unternehmen (Undertaking): In this historical context, the word carries a negative connotation of being ready for any plot, scheme, or unscrupulous deed because the person lacks a moral anchor.</term>

<summary>This page continues the moral instruction to a Prince, warning him against cynical advisors who claim that religion is merely a tool for controlling the masses. They bow and scrape; their faith consists only of grimaces, <note>In this context, "grimaces" (German: *Grimassen*) refers to exaggerated, theatrical displays of outward piety used to mask a lack of genuine belief.</note>

One pursues evil, blinded by appearances,
And believes oneself still virtuous even in vice.
One follows passion, and often has people
killed,
And then imagines one only needs to pray again.
The death-sweat that still drips from human brows
Is made right again if one runs into temples.
Prince! Vices that one would hardly trust to books
Were often committed by those who built churches.

--- Page 173 ---
<meta>Continuity: Following the previous page's warning about hypocritical bigots who use slander and psychological manipulation to control the Prince, the author now illustrates how history is full of tyrants who used outward religious displays to mask their cruelty. He contrasts this false piety with the Enlightenment ideal of a ruler whose faith is rooted in the love of humanity and sincere inner conviction.</meta>

Look back at history; often a barbarian,
A curse to all humanity, built the most magnificent altar,
And believed he could reconcile himself with God—
For the tears he extorted from humanity—once more upon his deathbed.
Flee the bigot, Prince! tolerate none around you;
For if you listen to him, oh Prince!—oh, then it is he who already rules.
In your name, he will murder and trample,
And then pray upon the corpses for those he has strangled.
Hold fast to this maxim, my Prince! the fall of a monarchy
Is caused by both libertinism <term>Freygeisterey (Libertinism/Freethinking): In the 18th century, this often referred to a radical rejection of religious and moral norms, which Enlightenment thinkers viewed as just as dangerous to social order as religious extremism.</term> and bigotry.
Be pious, my noble Prince! dedicate your early youth
To the love of humanity <term>Menschenliebe: A central Enlightenment concept referring to a universal, rational benevolence toward all people, regardless of status.</term>, for it is the most beautiful virtue.
A pious king is a god upon his throne,
Humanity’s happiness is his wish, their bliss is his reward.
With decorum you must, Prince! enter into holy temples,
Not babbling like a woman; <note>The author uses "plappern wie ein Weib" (babbling like a woman) to criticize mindless, repetitive ritual prayer, reflecting the gendered prejudices of the 18th century where emotional or unthinking behavior was often pejoratively labeled as feminine.</note> with decorum you must pray:
For books and singing—I tell you without hesitation—
Do not constitute true devotion; they are often merely hypocrisy.
The soul speaks to God; its plea pierces to the stars,
My Prince! yet the mouth must never distance itself from the heart.
Let truth be dear to you, my Prince! only she alone
Exalts kings and can be their protective shield.

<meta>Continuity: This page continues the poetic instruction to the Prince, transitioning from the previous page's focus on economic responsibility and the "Landesvater" (Father of the Land) ideal. It moves into the realm of judicial ethics and the inherent human equality that should guide a ruler's legal decisions.</meta>

Treat everyone well as your subject,
And see—you are a human yourself—him also as a
human.
Allow even the beggar in, and let him speak with
you,
And when you punish, my Prince! then punish the

And always spare the human, be lenient with his flaws;
Think: princes also fail, and princes are also weak.
Let your heart always defend the criminal's cause,
And if you must punish, then punish without revenge.
Always think: a prejudice makes people great and
small;
One honors the greatest thief, and locks the small one in.
<note>This couplet critiques the social hypocrisy of the era, where high-ranking individuals could commit massive financial or political "thefts" with impunity, while the poor were severely punished for petty survival crimes.</note>
Often, when a murderer dies, those who murder
thousands
Are rewarded by prejudice with ribbons and with medals.
<note>original: "Bändern und mit Orden" – refers to the sashes and medals given to military commanders. The author is likely referencing the Enlightenment critique of war as state-sanctioned mass murder.</note>
But without punishment, Prince! no crime can exist,
Only let reason always harmonize with the punishment.
If you let the small thief be hanged in your lands,
With what right can you grant freedom to the great?
Punishment strikes everyone equally; for everyone the same law;
There, status does not ennoble, and no lineage saves.
<note>This is a radical call for "Rechtsgleichheit" (equality before the law), a core Enlightenment principle that challenged the traditional legal privileges of the nobility.</note>
Prince! never give your power into the hands of the rich,
And may nobility never blind your heart!

<meta>This page continues the poetic advice to the Prince. The previous page ended with a warning that "nobility should never" be given absolute power over the poor or the law; this page clarifies that the ambitious aristocrat often hides his desire for power behind a mask of subservience.</meta>

He bows low before you; calls himself slave, subject,
And, if you overlook his nature, he becomes a tyrant.
In secret he envies you, Prince! for rank and greatness,
He always seeks your fall, and watches for every weakness.
Prince! be, I pray you, never too familiar with him:
The chance event that weakens you is what he builds upon.
Consult history. It has always been this way.
You can still read the truth in many a chronicle.
<note>The author uses "chronicle" to refer to historical records, suggesting that the patterns of political betrayal are a documented fact of human nature.</note>
The nobility’s greatness grows when that of the sovereign falls.
<note>original: "Des Adels Größe wächst, wenn die des Fürsten fällt." This reflects the perpetual power struggle between the central monarch and the landed aristocracy during the 18th century.</note>
So it has always gone, dear Prince! in the world.
There, where the strongest rules, the strong are servants,
But their hearts never forgive the loss of their self-proclaimed rights.
<note>This refers to the "traditional rights" or privileges that the nobility felt they were entitled to by birth, which often conflicted with the absolute power of a Prince.</note>
He seizes the opportunity, and if he then succeeds,
Then it is the tyrant who oppresses lands.

Prince! never value status; honor only the man,
And consider only the good <term>Unterthan</term> <note>Subject; a person living under the rule of a monarch.</note> to be great,
In whom you may trust, who hastens to meet you,
Sharing his heart with your well-being and your citizens.

<meta>This page continues the moral and political instructions to the Prince. The previous page concluded by warning the Prince not to surrender his power to the nobility, as an ambitious aristocrat can easily become a silent co-regent who oppresses the people.</meta>

Not he who counts ten thousand ancestors on parchment,
If loyalty and true virtue are missing from his heart.
<note>The reference to "parchment" refers to the literal animal skin used for noble patents and family trees, highlighting a critique of nobility based only on documents rather than character.</note>
He who is proud of his birth; arrogant in his rank,

Despite all this, my Prince! never forget loyalty,
And what the merit of true greatness may be.
Remember with gratitude what every person in the state
Has done for you, for the land and the people, and for the whole.
And consider that kings with their millions
Are never in a position to reward the <term>Ehrenmann</term> <note>A man of honor; a person of integrity who acts out of principle rather than for profit.</note>.

Then kings with their millions do not reward at all.
<note>The author emphasizes that while a King has vast financial resources, true moral worth or the service of a person of integrity cannot be bought with money; it must be motivated by internal virtue.</note>
Always seek out the noble man; remove the fool from the court,

When you are weary from your <term>Staatsgeschäfte</term> <note>Affairs of state; the official business of governing and administrative duties.</note>,
Then devote yourself to pleasure and draw new strength.

<meta>This page continues the moral and political education of the Prince, shifting from personal conduct and hunting to the ethical treatment of subjects, the horrors of unnecessary war, and the sanctity of diplomatic treaties.</meta>

In the cup of true pleasure, there are no human tears.
Enjoy the happiness, my Prince, that your heart gives you,
When every subject loves you, even the beggar.
Let rest and peace always dwell in your lands,
And learn the great art of always sparing <term>humanity</term><note>German: *Menschheit*. The author uses this term to emphasize a universal moral obligation that transcends social rank.</note>.

If humanity is dear to you, then spare human blood.

Unless it is done to protect the land and the people;
For the <term>fatherland</term><note>German: *Vaterland*. In the 18th-century context, this refers to the collective state and the mutual bond of loyalty between the ruler and the people.</note>, for you, everyone in the state gladly gives

Every person, believe me, Prince! will gladly give his life for you,
And everything he has, for the well-being of the whole.
When you make treaties, Prince! with foreign princes,
Base them upon your word, and not upon the circumstances <note>original: *Zeit*, literally "time," implying the shifting political winds or temporary advantages</note> or power.
Consider the matter beforehand, and let your will be firm:
To the wicked man alone is his word of honor a game.
He alone scorns the holiness of words;
And punishable is the man who desecrates them through power.

<meta>This page continues the moral instruction of the Prince. While the previous page focused on the Prince's own word and honesty, this page addresses the danger of corrupt advisors who might abuse the Prince's authority and how to maintain a virtuous administration.</meta>

I give you my word, Prince! You shall see it yourself;
Men will not dare so easily to deceive you anymore.
Often it is only attempted; and when you show weakness,
Feeling the injustice of a matter, half-suspecting and half-silent,
Then the <term>transgressor</term><note>German: *Frevler*. Refers to someone who commits a crime against moral or divine law; here, a corrupt courtier who abuses the sovereign's name.</note> dares it in your holy name,
Boldly relying on your leniency to condemn the innocent;
And when the cry then reaches your ears,
And the call of innocence soars up to the throne,
Then the evil worm stays silent, and smiles in the stillness,
And says, when you ask him: Prince! it was your
will.

My will? You shall then speak with a threatening face,
My will, monster! it was not my will.
I only trusted in you; and if I have been deceived,
Then my heart never failed, and I was only lied to.
And you, who deceived me, and dishonored my court,
You are a <term>villain</term><note>German: *Bösewicht*. A person of malicious character; in this context, an official who has betrayed the public trust and the Prince's honor.</note>, and worthy of my punishment.

The burden of the crown, Prince! shall not bewilder you;
It is not always hard to govern men,
If one desires what is good. Never lose your courage;
Prince! be a friend to <term>virtue</term><note>German: *Tugend*. A central concept in Enlightenment political theory, meaning the active pursuit of moral excellence and the well-being of the state.</note>, and you already govern
well.

Fill the highest offices in the land at all times
With the virtuous, Prince! then you will never be in 
want.

--- Page 184 ---
<meta>This page continues the poetic instruction of the Prince, moving from the responsibility of signing documents to the personal dangers of courtly sycophants. It explores the psychological isolation of a ruler surrounded by people who refuse to speak the truth.</meta>

They think as others think; those without a mind of their own,
Governed now by a servant, now by a <term>mistress</term><note>German: *Buhlerinn*. This refers to a female lover or favorite at court who exerts unofficial political power over the ruler.</note>.
They always bow low, never offering a noble contradiction,
And praise whatever is praised—even <term>vices</term><note>German: *Laster*. Moral failings or corrupt habits, presented here as the opposite of the "virtue" required of a ruler.</note> and crimes.
The night of <term>flattery</term><note>German: *Schmeicheley*. The author uses the metaphor of "night" to describe how flattery blinds a prince to the reality of his kingdom and his own character.</note>, which always surrounds princes,
Scarcely allows even the best of them to know who truly loves him.
And if equality alone can build the foundation of friendship,
How shall he find a friend instead of a <term>hypocrite</term>?
Believe me, my good Prince! An oath does not create a friend;
It grants loyalty only to servants, and rarely affection.
He whose heart alone chains him to your <term>virtue</term><note>German: *Tugend*. In Enlightenment philosophy, virtue was the essential quality of a "Great" person, consisting of rational self-control and concern for the common good.</note>—
He is the one who loves you, who saves you from vice.
Not the one who flatteringly tells you what you like to hear,
And who, following your every wish, gives you only what you want;
Who, crawling like a worm with a deeply bowed brow,
Ogles every servant and smiles at every wench.
Believe me, Prince! The pure light of <term>truth</term> never shines for him
Before whom everything is disguised, and whom no one contradicts;
Who errs majestically, and only sees what does not flee from him
By the twilight of a weak glimmer.

<summary>This page warns the Prince that flattery creates a "night" of deception, making it nearly impossible for a ruler to distinguish true friends from hypocrites. It argues that a true friend is one who is bound by virtue rather than a formal oath and who is willing to contradict the Prince to save him from vice.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the poetic moral instruction of the Prince. Following the previous page's warning about the blinding nature of flattery and the rarity of true friendship for a ruler, the text now shifts to practical advice: the Prince must actively seek out the truth among the common people and the virtuous, rather than expecting it to find him at court.</meta>

161
L

It is easy for a <term>Prince</term><note>German: *Fürst*. A general term for a sovereign ruler.</note> to be deceived by a friend,
Because everything at court lies with feigned <term>virtue</term><note>German: *Tugend*. In Enlightenment thought, virtue was the essential character of a true leader, involving rational self-control and honesty.</note>.
Learn, Prince! as I have said, to endure the truth,
And never be ashamed to consult a man of virtue,
For who knows everything well? Consider that one is always
learning,
And that a vain pride always keeps wisdom at a distance.
The man, my Prince! is great, who does not build proudly upon
himself,
Who thinks, and deliberates, and tests, before he trusts.
Who, when he hears everything, always chooses the wisest course,
The man, my Prince! is great, because he only rarely fails.
He who lives in the hut — oh, if only he always dared! —
He would sometimes, Prince! have much to say to the sovereign.
Visit him often as a friend; speak with the poor man,
And listen to what he says with careful <term>consideration</term><note>German: *Ueberlegung*. This implies a deep, rational reflecting on facts rather than a hasty reaction.</note>.
The temple of truth stands in the shadow of lovely beech trees;
There is her dwelling-place, Prince! the prince himself must
seek her.

She does not come to court; there at the golden gate,
Even if she should arrive there, she is seldom let inside.
Rather, she lives for the most part in the abandoned huts,
And flees from the palace, and from corrupt customs.
Follow me, my Prince! descend to your subject,
For you do not dishonor yourself: divinity's most beautiful gift

<summary>This page advises a Prince to seek truth outside the corrupting influence of the court, specifically by visiting and listening to common subjects in their "huts." It emphasizes that a ruler's greatness comes from humility, constant learning, and the ability to endure honest, unvarnished truth.</summary>

--- Page 186 ---
<meta>This page serves as a cautionary portrait of the court environment, providing the "negative" example to complement the advice on page 161. While the subsequent page encourages the Prince to seek truth among the common people, this page explains why the truth is so hard to find within the palace walls, focusing on the deceptive nature of sycophants.</meta>

Those who think only as others think; those without their own mind,
Governed now by a servant, now by a <term>mistress</term><note>German: *Buhlerinn*. A woman who seeks favor or maintains a romantic relationship with a man of power, often implying a manipulative influence.</note>.

Those who are always deeply bowed, who never nobly contradict,
And who praise what is praised—even <term>vices</term><note>German: *Laster*. In the 18th century, "Laster" referred to habitual moral failings or corrupt habits, the opposite of rational virtue.</note> and crimes.

The night of <term>flattery</term><note>German: *Schmeicheley*. Enlightenment writers often characterized courtly flattery as a "fog" or "night" that prevented rulers from seeing reality.</note>, which always surrounds princes,
Hardly allows even the best to know who truly loves him.

And if equality alone can ground the foundation of friendship,
How shall he find a friend instead of a <term>hypocrite</term><note>German: *Heuchler*. One who feigns beliefs, feelings, or virtues they do not actually possess to gain favor.</note>?

Believe me, my good Prince! An oath does not create a friend;
It grants loyalty only to <term>servants</term><note>German: *Knechten*. Refers to those bound by duty or low social standing, implying a lack of the free will required for genuine, noble friendship.</note>, and rarely tenderness.

He whom the heart alone chains to your virtue,
He is the one who loves you, who saves you from vice.

Not he who flatteringly tells you what you love,
And who grants only what you want, following every wish.

He who, crawling like a worm with a deeply bowed brow,
Ogles every servant and smiles at every <term>wench</term><note>German: *Dirne*. Here used to describe women of low status or loose morals orbiting the court to gain influence.</note>.

Believe me, Prince! The pure light of truth never shines for him
To whom everything is disguised, and whom no one contradicts.

Who errs majestically, and who—seeing only what does not flee from him—
Perceives things only by the twilight of a weak glimmer.

<summary>This page warns the Prince against the pervasive flattery of the court, arguing that true friendship and truth cannot exist where everyone is subservient and deceptive. It defines a true friend as someone bound by virtue who is willing to contradict the ruler for his own good.</summary>
<keywords>flattery, court life, virtue, vice, friendship, hypocrisy, truth, prince, Enlightenment morality</keywords>

### Page 165

Filled with everything that tastes good to a gourmet:
Let the wide anteroom resound with the crowd
Of subjects who sell themselves to him.
In vain he puts on a mask upon his face;
Yet dark grief does not leave his brow.
In vain the courtiers may <term>bless</term><note>original: *benedeyen*. An archaic loanword from the Latin *benedicere*, meaning to praise, extol, or invoke a blessing upon someone.</note> him;
The cries of his subjects still pierce his ear.
If he is a glutton, does he enjoy any peace?
Often a whole country melts away in a single <term>ragout</term><note>A rich, highly seasoned meat stew. Here, it serves as a metaphor for extravagant courtly waste that consumes the wealth of the entire nation.</note>.
If he is a drunkard, and to drink his fill,
He dares to sell humanity for wine;
If as a libertine he devours the poor man’s <term>possessions</term><note>original: *Haab*. An archaic spelling of *Habe*, referring to a person's total property or worldly goods.</note>,
Robs him of his daughter, and dishonors his wife;
If he is a warrior, and never enjoys peace
Until human blood flows for him like water;
Consider, dear Prince! and tell me, do you believe
That such a prince shall ever enjoy happiness?
By contrast, look upon the image of the prince whose will
Watches over the happiness of humanity; see how a serene calm
Covers his wise brow, which no reproach ever clouds,
Because everything tells him that he loves mankind.
He wanders through the fields and needs no guard;
His defense is his heart, and under every roof
There lives a subject with love and courage,
Who joyfully sacrifices the last drop of blood for him.

<summary>This page concludes a moral comparison between a selfish tyrant—haunted by the suffering of his people despite his luxury—and a benevolent ruler who finds safety and happiness in his subjects' love.</summary>

He falls asleep among roses; with graceful images
His own heart will depict for him the happiness of the peoples.
So it is, my good Prince! He who is moved by <term>virtue</term><note>original: *Tugend*. In the 18th-century context, this implies not just personal morality but a civic duty to act for the common good.</note>,
Who wishes only to bring happiness; who governs like a father,

He alone deserves, my Prince, the crown of a world.
Though fame may honor proud victors far and wide,
Do not believe, my Prince, that they are truly happy.
If their land does not love them, think: no pleasure touches them;
Not even love, if it is born of compulsion.
The citizen's heart must attach itself to the <term>prince</term><note>original: *Fürsten*. A general term for a sovereign ruler.</note> of its own free will;
Only this chain shall never be broken by time.
Let the love of his <term>subjects</term><note>original: *Unterthanen*. Those living under the authority of the sovereign.</note> be his only desire,
For without it, my Prince, what would princes be?
Reflect, Prince, I beg you, upon what I tell you;
Remember it on many a gloomy day:
For even the throne is not always free from sorrow,
And grief and <term>melancholy</term><note>original: *Melancholey*. A deep, pensive, and long-lasting sadness, often considered a "noble" ailment in historical literature.</note> lurk even within the <term>royal purple</term><note>original: *Purpur*. Referring to the deep red or purple dyes historically reserved for the robes of emperors and kings.</note>.
You may feel the wound of every misfortune in your breast,
When everyone flees from you in that heavy hour,
When a prince's splendor and greatness are entirely forgotten,
And illness proves that he is only a man;
For when the eye sees the vanity of greatness,
And the approaching <term>pallor of death</term><note>original: *Todtenblässe*. The characteristic whiteness of a corpse; here used to show that death is the ultimate equalizer between a prince and a commoner.</note> bleaches even the royal purple,
Then, Prince, remember my words with kindness;
Die in peace, and the God of goodness will reward you there.

<summary>This page concludes a poetic mirror for princes, emphasizing that true kingly greatness comes from the love of one's citizens and a virtuous heart rather than military conquest. It ends with a memento mori, reminding the ruler that in death, his only comfort will be his good deeds and divine reward.</summary>

--- Page 195 ---
<meta>This page (167) shifts from the specific advice given to a "Prince" on the preceding pages to a more generalized philosophical meditation on human nature. While the previous page focused on the duties of a ruler, this page addresses humanity at large, arguing against the tendency to blame "fate" or "misfortune" for human suffering. It asserts that misery is the product of human folly and malice rather than external forces.</meta>

# Do not revile Fortune!
<sig>L</sig>

Mortals, do not revile <term>Fortune</term><note>original: *Glück*. In 18th-century German, this word encompasses both "luck" (fate) and "happiness." The poet is arguing that what we call "bad luck" is often just the result of poor choices.</note>! No, do not call it an <term>ill star</term><note>original: *Unstern*. A literal "evil star," referring to the astrological belief that one's destiny is dictated by the heavens.</note> when you—blind as a child on the leash of <term>folly</term><note>original: *Torheit*. A lack of good sense or moral judgment, often contrasted with Enlightenment ideals of reason.</note>—allow yourselves to be led about and run headlong into ruin. You deluded ones! You who choose evil because you mostly fail to recognize the good—cast an impartial glance of truth back upon yourselves; ashamed, you will see that misfortune lies only within yourselves; for you alone are at fault.

How foolish are the laments of men, with which they always revile fortune!—O mortals! one needs only to see you act to see where the blame for <term>misery</term><note>original: *Elends*. Refers to a state of great distress or poverty, presented here as a social and moral consequence rather than an act of God.</note> lies; it is you yourselves who plague yourselves and others: the man who makes war on man and beast is also his own enemy. There need be no misfortune in nature that disturbs your peace; it is you yourselves, beguiled by a <term>malicious mind</term><note>original: *Bosheitssinn*. A disposition toward evil, spite, or ill-will.</note>, who rob yourselves of happiness and peace, who hate and persecute one another, and who always believe you can find the source of your own <term>well-being</term><note>original: *Wohls*. General prosperity, health, or "the common good."</note> only in the tears of your brothers.

<vocab>Fortune/Happiness, Ill star, Folly, Ruin, Misfortune, Misery, Maliciousness, Tears, Well-being</vocab>

<summary>This page contains a moralizing poem that challenges the reader to take responsibility for their own suffering rather than blaming fate or "bad luck." It emphasizes that human malice and folly are the true sources of worldly misery.</summary>
<keywords>ethics, free will, folly, human nature, Enlightenment, social responsibility, fate</keywords>

--- Page 196 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical argument from page 167, shifting from a general critique of those who blame "Fortune" to a direct appeal for self-reflection. The author maintains that the universe is inherently good and that human misery is a self-inflicted consequence of moral blindness.</meta>

O learn to know yourselves, learn first to know your heart,
here you find the seed of evil,
the source of your sufferings.
All of <term>creation</term><note>original: *Schöpfung*. In this context, it refers to the physical world as a manifestation of divine harmony and order.</note> offers you joys
and bliss in abundance;
benevolently it always keeps evil hidden from you,
and opens to you the wealth of heavenly delights.
But your eye, clouded by <term>prejudices</term><note>original: *Vorurtheilen*. A key Enlightenment term referring to irrational beliefs or traditional dogmas that obscure the "light" of reason.</note>,
does not perceive it.
Let <term>universal philanthropy</term><note>original: *allgemeine Menschenliebe*. An Enlightenment ideal of selfless, cosmopolitan love for all of humankind, often viewed as the foundation of true morality.</note>
first animate your actions,
clear your eye, so that you see brightly,
then you will not lack true <term>happiness</term><note>original: *Glücke*. This term encompasses both outward "fortune" and inward "joy."</note>,
for which you now plead in vain.
Do not seek the enemy in <term>Nature</term><note>original: *Natur*. Here personified as a nurturing, motherly force rather than a source of "natural disasters" or "evil."</note>
who is contrary to you;
All of creation is your friend,
and loves you tenderly, as a mother
loves her child; be only certain
that when men are <term>miserable</term><note>original: *elend*. Refers to a state of wretchedness or distress.</note>,
the blame is borne by no being
whom you call your enemy.
If you are unhappy, then accuse
yourselves, and if you run into ruin,
do not say that a foreign hand
led you to the edge
of the abyss; that an <term>evil being</term><note>original: *böses Wesen*. A reference to the Devil or personified supernatural malevolence, which the author argues is an excuse for human failing.</note>
cast you down; no! it was you yourselves.

<note>Vocabulary: Love of humanity, Creation, Prejudices, Nature, Happiness/Fortune</note>

<summary>This page concludes a poetic meditation on human responsibility, arguing that the world is inherently benevolent and that suffering stems from internal prejudices rather than external fate or evil spirits. It advocates for self-knowledge and universal love as the keys to happiness.</summary>
<keywords>self-reflection, philanthropy, Enlightenment philosophy, nature, human agency, prejudice, happiness</keywords>

--- Page 197 ---
<meta>This page continues the moral exhortation from page 168. And you, o youths! you who
in your first strength, full of spirit and <term>ambition for glory</term><note>original: *Ruhmbegier*. A desire for fame or distinction, often viewed in Enlightenment thought as a double-edged sword that can lead to either greatness or vanity.</note>
strive to reach the summit,
toward which your bold gaze already rises;
you who, inflamed by wild <term>youthful heat</term><note>original: *Jugendhiz'*. Refers to the impetuousness and passion of young age.</note>,
in life’s cradle-years,
as yet unacquainted with the world and men
and the dangers that threaten you there,
let yourselves be carried away, now toward the good and now toward the evil,
with the same <term>flight of enthusiasm</term><note>original: *Schwärmerflug*. In this period, "Schwärmerei" often implied an irrational, over-excited, or fanatical state of mind.</note>;
Trust not your senses; they are very often deceivers.
If <term>Fortune</term><note>original: *Glück*. Here personified as a fickle goddess. The term encompasses both outward "luck" and inward "happiness."</note> has chosen you as its favorites,
take heed of your time,
and make use of every second.
It is often a single <term>moment</term><note>original: *Augenblick*. Literally an "eye-blink"; a common trope emphasizing how quickly a life's path can change.</note> that makes our fortune,
and quickly it is gone,
and with it our happiness; and alas, the consequences are
often the destiny of our entire lives.
It is no longer in our power to pre-
->vent<-
them, and neither tears nor pleas can change
<term>fate’s decree</term><note>original: *Schicksals Schluß*. The final, unalterable decision of destiny.</note>.
Conduct yourselves with modesty when in Fortune's favor;
she is a woman, and withdraws just as quickly
as she was once near you;
gladly she offers you her hand,
yet no sooner do you anger her than her rosy cheek 
already glows <note>The "glow" here suggests the reddening of the face in anger.</note>, for even goddesses are
not free from passion,

**Vocabulary**
Youths, Ambition for glory, Youthful heat, Cradle-years, Flight of enthusiasm, Fortune, Moment, Fate's decree, Rosy cheek, Goddesses

<summary>The author warns young men that their impulsive passions and the fleeting nature of time can lead to lifelong consequences. Fortune is personified as a fickle goddess who requires modest behavior and careful attention to keep her favor.</summary>
<keywords>youth, ambition, Fortune (Glück), morality, personification, Enlightenment didacticism, passion</keywords>
