<meta>This page continues the moral instruction to youth regarding the dangers of unguided passion. While the previous page ended with an exhortation to "conduct" oneself properly, this page emphasizes the need for a mentor or friend to anchor the spirit against the flighty illusions of the imagination.</meta>

and bear a human <term>heart</term><note>original: *Gemüth*. A term central to German Enlightenment thought, referring to the seat of a person's emotions, character, and inner soul.</note>.
Therefore, learn quickly the difficult art
of maintaining the love of the <term>goddess</term><note>original: *Göttinn*. Likely referring to Lady Fortune, whose favor the author previously warned was fickle.</note>,
the art over which the <term>ancients</term><note>original: *die Alten*. Referring to the philosophers of Greek and Roman antiquity.</note> already
racked their brains, and which few understand.
Never forget to realize clearly
how necessary the guidance of a friend is to you,
who leads you on the right paths,
admonishes you when you falter,
who prepares the way before you,
holds you back from evil, and always drives you toward the good.
Otherwise, you will never succeed
in enjoying true <term>happiness</term><note>original: *Glücks*. This word denotes both "luck" (external fortune) and "happiness" (internal state).</note>;
in vain will you soar upward,
and you will always remain in the foul swamp.
Restrain your spirit with deliberation
when it wishes to carry you away, 
with the sun’s rapid course, 
into the realm of airy <term>chimeras</term><note>original: *Schimären*. Mythological monsters used here as a metaphor for wild, impossible fantasies or delusions.</note>; 
the path is flowery: but
to return from there to reality,
and to bring back from the land of <term>reveries</term><note>original: *Träumerey'n*. Often used in this period to warn against "enthusiasm" or "fanaticism" (Schwärmerei) that lacks a basis in practical reality.</note>
nothing at all
but wishes, fantasies,
and ideals of <term>guardian spirits</term><note>original: *Genien*. In Roman mythology, the *genius* was a protective spirit; here, it refers to idealized, supernatural concepts of perfection.</note>,
that hurts too much, and is not worth the effort
of wearying the wings of the spirit 
for such a cause; moreover, we often
lack the strength to pull back the reins,
and if one lets it go, like <term>Phaeton</term><note>original: *Phaeten*. In Greek mythology, Phaeton attempted to drive the chariot of the sun but lost control, nearly destroying the earth before being struck down by Zeus. He is a classic symbol of youthful hubris and lack of self-control.</note>,

<summary>This page warns young people about the dangers of excessive imagination and "airy chimeras," advocating instead for the steady guidance of a wise friend. It uses the myth of Phaeton to illustrate the catastrophe that occurs when youthful spirit is not restrained by reason.</summary>

<keywords>mentorship, imagination vs. reality, Lady Fortune, Phaeton, moral guidance, Enlightenment philosophy, chimeras, human spirit</keywords>

--- Page 199 ---
<meta>This page continues the moral instruction from the previous page, specifically concluding the warning against wild imagination and transitioning into a meditation on contentment and the true nature of human merit. The catchword "die" from the previous page connects to the first line here.</meta>

the rapid <term>sun-horses</term><note>original: *Sonnenpferde*. A classical allusion to the chariot of Helios or Apollo, symbolizing the fast-moving, often uncontrollable flight of the imagination.</note> rule,
then it is done; heart and mind
they hurry away with them,
and constantly wander in <term>fairyland</term><note>original: *Feenland*. Used here as a derogatory term for a state of being lost in unrealistic illusions or daydreams.</note>.
Do not despise a moderate happiness;
contentment with little,
always a cheerful, bright gaze,
which announces our soul’s peace,
this is the wise man's <term>bliss</term><note>original: *Seligkeit*. Refers to a state of profound spiritual happiness or "blessedness."</note>.
One is often, in possession of great goods
and high rank, no happier
than the poorest herder in the field,
who, with cheese and bread, wishes for nothing more
than a clear sky.

### True Greatness.

A person who thinks too much of themselves
betrays their own <term>exposure</term><note>original: *Blöße*. Literally "nakedness," but here meaning one's flaws, weaknesses, or lack of inner substance.</note>:
For not in nobility, rank, and money
does human greatness consist.

Diplomas do not make us great,
nor do they ennoble our lives:
He reveals himself as a fool
who believes that they elevate him.

<summary>This page concludes a poetic warning against overactive imagination, advocating instead for the "wise man's bliss" found in simple contentment, and begins a new section arguing that true greatness comes from character rather than wealth or titles.</summary>
<keywords>contentment, moderation, true greatness, nobility, virtue vs. status, imagination, enlightenment ethics</keywords>

--- Page 200 ---
<meta>This page contains the poetic verses corresponding to the theme of "True Greatness" introduced at the end of the previous page. It expands on the Enlightenment-era argument that moral character and merit are personal achievements rather than hereditary rights.</meta>

-> True greatness is a good; <-
-> one must acquire it for oneself: <-
-> One cannot, through rank and blood, <-
-> inherit it without a will. <note>original: *ab intestato*. A legal Latin term referring to the distribution of an estate when someone dies without leaving a valid will; here used metaphorically to mean greatness cannot be passed down automatically.</note> <-

-> And even if the father was a hero, <-
-> like none who had ever been; <-
-> the son might inherit his money, <-
-> but not his noble nature. <-

-> How many did not fall in battle, <-
-> dedicating themselves to the fatherland; <-
-> and their children, their lineage, <-
-> are the disgrace of their glory. <-

-> Be human, be devoted to virtue; <-
-> ennoble your deeds, <-
-> Give for the sovereign <note>original: *Fürsten*. While often translated as "prince," in this context it refers more broadly to the ruling monarch or the state.</note> life and blood, <-
-> be men, be soldiers. <-
